Zigzags and free adjunctions
Lorenzo Riva, Martina Rovelli

Abstract
. We construct an explicit combinatorial model of the functor which adds right adjoints to the morphisms of an ∞ \infty -category, and we speculate on possible extensions to higher dimensions.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation: adding adjoints

Fix an
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathscr{C}
and two
k
-morphisms
f:x\to y
and
g:y\to x
in
\mathscr{C}
between parallel
(k-1)
-morphisms
x
and
y
. Then
f
is a
left adjoint
of
g
(and
g
is a
right adjoint
of
f
) if there are
(k+1)
-morphisms
\eta:\mathrm{id}(x)\to g\circ_{k}f
and
\varepsilon:f\circ_{k}g\to\mathrm{id}(y)
in
\mathscr{C}
satisfying the snake equations

(\varepsilon\circ_{k}\mathrm{id}(f))\circ_{k+1}(\mathrm{id}(f)\circ_{k}\eta)\simeq\mathrm{id}(f),\qquad(\mathrm{id}(g)\circ_{k}\varepsilon)\circ_{k+1}(\eta\circ_{k}\mathrm{id}(g))\simeq\mathrm{id}(g).

If, in addition,
\mathscr{C}
is symmetric monoidal, then we can similarly define a notion of
left/right adjoints
(more commonly called
duals
) for objects.

Given any homotopy commutative monoid
S
(i.e.
S
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in the
\infty
-category
\mathfrak{S}
of spaces) we can ask the following question: what is the smallest symmetric monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(S)
such that (1)
S
is contained in the space of objects of
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(S)
and (2) for
0\leq k<n
, all
k
-morphisms of
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(S)
have a left and right adjoint? Notice that this is a purely categorical property, so it is very surprising that the answer, at least in a special case, appears to be purely geometric: the
cobordism hypothesis

[
BD95
,
Lur09
]
asserts that, when
S=A^{1}
is generated by a single element,
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(A^{1})
is equivalent to the
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}
whose
k
-morphisms are
n
-framed compact
k
-manifolds with corners. A reference for this statement can be found in
[
Lur09
]
and the arguments depend crucially on decomposing of manifolds into suitable handles, each of which witnesses an adjunction.

We can imagine a different strategy: first build
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(S)
for any
S
with purely categorical or combinatorial tools, and then exhibit an explicit equivalence
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(A^{1})\simeq\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}
, thus bypassing the need to show that
\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}
has the desired universal property. Variations of this strategy have been used in dimensions
n\leq 3
for this and similar problems – see, for example,
[
Abr96
,
SP09
,
Juh18
]
– but they rely on our understanding of low-dimensional manifolds. Nevertheless, it is interesting to think whether we can exploit some
partial
knowledge of high-dimensional manifolds, like their handle decompositions, to make this strategy work. The question then turns to: how do we build
F^{n,\mathrm{adj}}(S)
?

In this paper we answer this question in the slightly different context of freely adding right adjoints to the morphisms of an
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathscr{C}
. We can describe an
(\infty,2)
-category
F^{2,\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{C})

informally as follows:

(0)

its objects are the objects of
\mathscr{C}
;

(1)

its
1
-morphisms are formal zigzags of
1
-morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
, with composition given by concatenation;

(2)

its
2
-morphisms are generated under vertical composition by formal zigzags of commutative squares in
\mathscr{C}
, subject to the condition that the first and last vertical legs of each zigzags are invertible, with a minimal set of relations.

For example, given any
1
-morphism
f
of
\mathscr{C}
, pictured as an arrow
f=(x\xlongrightarrow{f}y)
, there should be a corresponding
1
-morphism
f^{\prime}
going in the opposite direction of
f
, pictured as
f^{\prime}=(y\xlongleftarrow{f}x)
, and since we are enforcing that
F^{2,\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{C})
be an
(\infty,2)
-category we must also have composites of the form

g^{\prime}\circ_{1}f=(x\xlongrightarrow{f}y\xlongleftarrow{g}x),\qquad h\circ_{1}g^{\prime}\circ_{1}f=(x\xlongrightarrow{f}y\xlongleftarrow{g}x\xlongrightarrow{h}y),

and so on. The generating
2
-morphisms of
F^{n,\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{C})
are of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{5}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{4}}

where each individual square is a commutative square in
\mathscr{C}
and the left and right vertical morphisms are invertible. In particular, given each
f
in
\mathscr{C}
we have two
2
-morphisms

\eta:=\,\hbox to120.98pt{\vbox to45.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.48946pt\lower-24.22081pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where the first equivalence is obtained by composing the diagram vertically and the second by composing it horizontally (we can do this since
2
-morphisms in an
(\infty,2)
-category satisfy the
interchange law
), and the other snake equation is similar. Constructions of this type have appeared in the literature to answer similar questions about adding adjoints – see, for example, Dawson-Paré-Pronk’s work on strict
2
-categories of fences
[
DPP03
]
or Cnossen-Lez-Linskens’ work on span
2
-categories
[
CLL25
]
– or inverses – see Dwyer-Kan’s hammock localization of simplicial categories
[
DK80
]
or the general calculus of fractions
[
GZ67
]
. Moreover, using work of Loubaton-Ruit
[
LR25
]
on the universal property of the double
\infty
-category of squares in
\mathscr{C}
, it’s not surprising to find that the
(\infty,2)
-category that we described above also has some kind of universal property.

1.2.
Main results

Describing an
(\infty,2)
-category using its
k
-morphisms hides a lot of data and does not make clear whether the construction accounts for higher coherence data. The first main result is that this informal description can be upgraded to a formal one:

Theorem A

(
3.27
,
4.9
,
4.10
)
.

There is a functor
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}:\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
such that, for any
\mathscr{C}\in\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
,

(0)

the space of objects of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
is the space of objects of
\mathscr{C}
;

(1)

the space of
1
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
is the space of formal zigzags of
1
-morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
, with composition given by concatenation;

(2)

the space of
2
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
is generated under vertical composition by the space of formal zigzags of commutative squares in
\mathscr{C}
, subject to the condition that the first and last vertical legs of each zigzags are invertible, with a minimal set of relations.

Moreover, there is an inclusion
i:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
(natural in
\mathscr{C}
) which sends all
1
-morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
to
1
-morphisms with a right adjoint in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
.

Secondly, we show that
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
is universal with respect to that property:

Theorem B

(
4.14
,
4.15
)
.

Consider the functor
\tau_{1}(-)^{\mathrm{ladj}}:\mathfrak{Cat}_{2}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{1}
which computes the sub-
\infty
-category containing only the
1
-morphisms which are left adjoints. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}
is a left adjoint of
\tau_{1}(-)^{\mathrm{ladj}}
, with the inclusion
\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C})
as the unit: for any
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{D}
,

\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{C}),\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\mathscr{C},\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ladj}}).

In particular there is an equivalence
\mathcal{Z}^{2}([1])\simeq\mathrm{Adj}
, where
[1]
is the walking
1
-morphism and
\mathrm{Adj}
is the walking adjunction.

In the discussion following
4.15
we show why the above theorem also has a surprisingly geometric analogue using the well-known geometric formulation of the walking adjunction
\mathrm{Adj}
from
[
RV16
]
.

This zigzag construction can be extended to higher dimensions. Indeed, the functor
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}
is part of a family of functors
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}:\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n+1)}
such that the
k
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
can be similarly described as zigzags – see
3.27
. We show that each
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}
adds right adjoints:

Theorem C

(
5.4
)
.

Fix
n\geq 1
. For any
\mathscr{C}\in\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
there is a map
\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
which, for
1\leq k\leq n
, sends every
k
-morphism in
\mathscr{C}
to a
k
-morphism which has a right adjoint in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
.

The problem of whether
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
is initial with respect to this property remains undecided, but we expect this partial result:

Conjecture D

(
5.8
)
.

Fix
n\geq 1
. For any
1\leq k\leq n+1
, the space of
k
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(\mathscr{C})
is generated under composition by the
k
-morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
and the adjunction (co)units for lower-dimensional morphisms of
X
.

1.3.
Future work

Ultimately we hope to connect our zigzag construction with the cobordism hypothesis. Here we sketch our strategy and our hopes for future results. Consider
F^{k}
, the free
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-algebra in spaces on a single (non-identity) generator. Using the stabilization hypothesis (see, for example,
[
Hau18
, Proposition 10.11]
),
F^{k}
induces an
(\infty,k)
-category
B^{k}F^{k}
with a contractible space of
j
-morphisms for all
0\leq j<k
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+n}(B^{k}F^{k})
is an
(\infty,k+n)
- category with a contractible space of
j
-morphisms for all
0\leq j<k
, and thus it induces an
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category
T^{k,n}:=\Omega^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+n}(B^{k}F^{k})
. Our results tell us that
T^{k,n}
has left adjoints for all
j
-morphisms when
0\leq j\leq n-1
, and
5.5
further ensures that those left adjoints are also right adjoints when
j\leq n-2
. This is a similar property to that of the
oriented tangle category

\mathrm{Tang}_{k,n}^{\mathrm{or}}
, an
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category constructed in
[
AF17
]
whose
j
-morphisms are compact oriented
j
-manifolds with corners with an embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{k+j}
.

Conjecture E

(
5.9
)
.

For any
k,n,\geq 1
there exists a map
T^{k,n}\to\mathrm{Tang}_{k,n}^{\mathrm{or}}
sending the generator
\ast\in F^{k}\subseteq T^{k,n}
to the object
(\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k})\in\mathrm{Tang}^{\mathrm{or}}_{k,n}
.

At the end of
Section

5
we give an idea of how this map can be constructed. Taking colimits as
k\to\infty
we would obtain a map
\colim_{k}T^{k,n}\to\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}
, the oriented cobordism category. We hope that we can use this map to get a better grasp on the latter
(\infty,n)
-category and on the oriented cobordism hypothesis – in particular, we’d like to investigate how close this map is to being an equivalence.

1.4.
Organization of the paper and notation

While our main results are framed in the language of
(\infty,n)
-categories, the technical work is done with higher Segal spaces; to get back the results one just has to start with a higher Segal space that is, in addition, complete. We start by recalling some categorical notions in
Section

2
, mostly concerning the theory of higher Segal spaces. We prove a general formula that extracts a globular
n
-uple Segal space (equivalently, a
2
-fold Segal space) from any
n
-simplicial space, and we use that formula in
Section

3
to construct the zigzagification functor
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n}
. In
Section

4
we prove the universal property of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}
by exploiting some results about generating spaces of
2
-morphisms. Finally, in
Section

5
we discuss the general case and talk about future work on the connection with the tangle hypothesis.

We work mostly model independently. For each
n
we fix an
\infty
-category
\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
of
(\infty,n)
-categories and set
\mathfrak{S}:=\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,0)}
, which we call the
\infty
-category of spaces. There is a truncation functor
\tau_{m}:\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,m)}
for each
m\leq n
which is a right adjoint to the canonical inclusion
\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,m)}\subseteq\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
. In any
(\infty,n)
-category we have composition operations
-\circ_{k}-
for each
1\leq k\leq n
(where the subscript
k
denotes that we are composing in the
k
th direction, i.e. along a shared
(k-1)
-morphism) and an identity operation
\mathrm{id}(-)
which takes a
k
-morphism to its identity
(k+1)
-morphism. We use
\partial^{0}
and
\partial^{1}
to denote the source and target operations, respectively. We use
(-)^{\mathrm{op}_{i}}:\mathfrak{Cat}_{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{n}
to denote the functor switching the source and target of
i
-morphisms.

We denote by
\mathbf{\Delta}
the (ordinary) category of simplices, whose objects
[t]\in\mathbf{\Delta}
are the ordered sets
{0<1<\dotsb<t}
and whose morphisms are the order-preserving functions. This category comes with a canonical map
\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{1}
and we tend to think of
[t]
as its image under this map. An object of the product
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
is denoted by
[\underline{t}]
, where
\underline{t}=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})
is an
n
-tuple of non-negative integers. Tuples of the form
(a,\dotsc,a)
will be abbreviated as
\underline{a^{n}}
. We write
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}
for
(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

We kindly thank Chris Schommer-Pries, Stephan Stolz, Dan Freed, and Tomer Schlank for enlightening conversations about this work. The first author is generously supported by the Simons Foundation on Global Categorical Symmetries (SFI-MPS-GCS-00008528-09) and the Center for Mathematical Sciences and Applications.

2.
Categorical preliminaries

We start by recalling some of the theory of higher Segal spaces and their specializations all the way to complete globular
n
-uple Segal spaces. The only original results are in
Section

2.3
and serve a purely technical (though vital) role in the paper, so the readers who are familiar with higher Segal spaces should feel free to skip to the next section and refer back to the new results when needed.

2.1.
Higher Segal spaces

Fix an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{A}
with finite limits.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Segal object in
\mathscr{A}
is a functor
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{A}
such that, for every
n
, the canonical projection map
X_{n}\to X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}
induced by the inert inclusions
[1]\hookrightarrow[n]
is an equivalence; this is referred to as the
Segal condition
. We denote by
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{A})
the full sub-
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
spanned by the Segal objects in
\mathscr{A}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Limits in
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{A})
are computed pointwise. In particular,
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{A})
has all finite limits.

Definition 2.3
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
of
n
-uple Segal objects in
\mathscr{A}
is defined inductively as follows:

•

\mathrm{Seg}^{0}(\mathscr{A}):=\mathscr{A}
;

•

\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{A}):=\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{A})
;

•

\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A}):=\mathrm{Seg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A}))
for
n\geq 2
.

We will mostly concentrate on the case
\mathscr{A}=\mathfrak{S}
, for which we now set up some terminology. An object
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
is called an
n
-uple Segal space
. By unravelling the definition we see that an
n
-uple Segal space
X
is an
n
-simplicial space
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}\to\mathfrak{S}
satisfying the Segal condition in each variable independently. We call
X_{\underline{t}}:=X([\underline{t}])
the
space of
\underline{t}
-morphisms
of
X
. It will be helpful to call
elementary
those tuples
\underline{t}
consisting only of
0
s and
1
s, and for elementary tuples their
dimension
is the sum
\sum_{i}t_{i}
(equivalently, the number of entries of
\underline{t}
equal to
1
).

The Segal condition allows us to define composition operations among
\underline{t}
-morphisms in
X
. For
n=1
, for example, we have a span

X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}\xlongleftarrow{\simeq}X_{2}\to X_{1}

where the second map is induced by the active inclusion
[1]\cong\{0,2\}\hookrightarrow[2]
. We denote the resulting map
X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}\to X_{1}
by
-\circ_{1}-
. Similarly, for
n\geq 2
and any
i
we have a span

X_{\bullet,\dotsc,1,\dotsc\bullet}\times_{X_{\bullet,\dotsc,0,\dotsc\bullet}}X_{\bullet,\dotsc,1,\dotsc\bullet}\xlongleftarrow{\simeq}X_{\bullet,\dotsc,2,\dotsc\bullet}\to X_{\bullet,\dotsc,1,\dotsc\bullet}

induced by the same maps as before but in the
i
th factor of
\mathbf{\Delta}
, whose composite we denote by
-\circ_{i}-
. All these “composition” operations are appropriately unital and associative, and the associativity includes relations between compositions in different directions. The units are induced by the maps
\pi:[1]\to[0]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
; the operation of precomposing by
\pi
in the
i
th factor is denoted by
\mathrm{id}_{i}(-)
and increases the dimension of a morphism by
1
. We also have boundary operations, induced by the inclusions
\theta^{0},\theta^{1}:[0]\to[1]
; precomposing by
\theta^{l}
in the
i
th factor is denoted by
\partial_{i}^{l}
, and this operation lowers the dimension by
1
. We call
\partial_{i}^{0}(f)
and
\partial_{i}^{1}(f)
the
source and target of
f
in the
i
th direction
, and we call
\mathrm{id}_{i}(f)
the
identity of
f
in the
i
th direction
.

2.2.
Globularity

We are going to introduce some extra conditions that one can impose on multisimplicial objects. Once again, we fix an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{A}
with finite limits.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{E}
be a functor of
\infty
-categories with a right adjoint
G
, and let
\mathscr{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{E}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
be full sub-
\infty
-categories such that
F_{0}:=F|_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}
factors through
\mathscr{E}_{0}
. If
G_{0}:=G|_{\mathscr{E}_{0}}
factors through
\mathscr{D}_{0}
then
G_{0}
is a right adjoint of
F_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
d\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
and
e\in\mathscr{E}_{0}
. Then

\mathscr{E}_{0}(F_{0}(d),e)\simeq\mathscr{E}(F_{0}(d),e)\simeq\mathscr{D}(d,G(e))\simeq\mathscr{D}(d,G_{0}(e))\simeq\mathscr{D}_{0}(d,G_{0}(e))

where the last equivalence holds because, by assumption,
G_{0}(e)\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
i:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}}\simeq\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}
denote the functor
[\underline{t}]\mapsto[\underline{t},0]
. The evaluation functor
\mathrm{ev}_{0}:=i^{\ast}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
given by
X\mapsto X_{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet,0}
admits a left and a right adjoint:

(L)

its left adjoint
i_{!}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
sends
X
to the functor
[\underline{t},u]\mapsto X_{\underline{t}}
which is constant in the last variable;

(R)

its right adjoint
i_{\ast}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
sends
X
to the functor
[\underline{t},u]\mapsto(X_{\underline{t}})^{\times(u+1)}
, with projections as face maps and diagonals as degeneracy maps.

The left adjoint is fully faithful. Moreover, the evaluation functor restricts to
\mathrm{ev}_{0}:\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})
and the adjunctions
i_{!}\dashv\mathrm{ev}_{0}\dashv i_{\ast}
restrict to adjunctions at the level of higher Segal objects.

Proof.

The object
[0]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
is initial, so
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
is simply the functor which takes the limit in the last factor of
\mathbf{\Delta}
; therefore (L) follows. From the formula we see that the unit of
i_{!}\dashv\mathrm{ev}_{0}
is an equivalence, implying that
i_{!}
is fully faithful. For (R) we just have to show that we can right Kan extend along
i
: indeed, the pointwise formula for right Kan extensions gives us

(\mathrm{RKan}_{i}(X))_{\underline{t},u}\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\underline{t},u]\to[\underline{s},0]\\
\text{in }\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\end{subarray}}X_{\underline{s}}\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}[0]\to[u]\\
\text{in }\mathbf{\Delta}\end{subarray}}X_{\underline{t}}\simeq X_{\underline{t}}^{u+1},

where the limit exists since we assumed that
\mathscr{A}
had all finite limits.

The formulas show that
\mathrm{ev}_{0}X
,
i_{!}X
, and
i_{\ast}X
satisfy the Segal conditions in all coordinates whenever
X
does, so all these functors restrict appropriately. The last claim follows using
2.4
since
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
is a full sub-
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
.
∎

Since the left adjoint
i_{!}
is fully faithful we have a diagram of inclusions of full sub-
\infty
-categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Seg}^{2}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}

Definition 2.6
.

We say that a functor
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{k,\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{A}
is
constant
if it belongs to the sub-
\infty
-category
\mathscr{A}
, or equivalently if the canonical map
X_{0,\dotsc,0}\to X_{\underline{t}}
is an equivalence for every
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{k}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
1\leq k\leq n
. We say that
X\in\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
is
globular at height
k
if, for every
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{k-1}
, the
(n-k-1)
-simplicial object
X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}}
is constant. We say that
X
is
globular up to height
k
if it globular at heights
1,2,\dotsc,k
. Denote by
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
the full sub-
\infty
-category spanned by the
n
-simplicial objects which are globular up to height
k
and by
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
the further sub-
\infty
-category spanned by the
n
-uple Segal objects which are globular up to height
k
. If
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
we just say that it is
globular
.

Remark 2.8
.

If
X\in\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
, to check if it is globular at height
k
it is enough to verify the constancy condition for elementary tuples
\underline{t}
, i.e. those containing only
0
s and
1
s.

If
X
is globular up to dimension
k
then it is globular up to dimension
k-1
, so we have a diagram of inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n-1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n-2}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n-1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n-2}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Glb}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Note also that every
n
-simplicial object is globular at height
n
, since the
0
-uple Segal object
X_{\underline{t},0}
, being just objects of
\mathscr{A}
, are always constant. Thus the top left and bottom left horizontal maps in the diagram are equalities.

After unpacking the definition, we see that an
n
-uple Segal space which is globular up to height
k
is determined by the data of

•

spaces
X_{\underline{0^{n}}}
,
X_{1,\underline{0^{n-1}}}
,
X_{1,1,\underline{0^{n-2}}}
,
\dotsc
,
X_{\underline{1^{k}},\underline{0^{n-k}}}
of
k
-morphisms
for
0\leq k\leq i
,

•

an
(n-k)
-uple Segal space
X_{\underline{1^{k}},\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}
whose
(0,\dotsc,0)
-morphisms are the
k
-morphisms of
X
,

•

appropriately unital and associative composition operations.

It turns out that globular
n
-uple Segal objects are also known by a different name. Consider the
\infty
-category
S^{n}(\mathscr{A})
of
n
-fold Segal objects in
\mathscr{A}
(see
[
Hau18
, Definition 4.4]
) defined inductively as follows:

•

S^{0}(\mathscr{A}):=\mathscr{A}
,

•

S^{1}(\mathscr{A}):=\mathrm{Seg}(\mathscr{A})
,

•

S^{n}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}(S^{n-1}(\mathscr{A}))
is the full subcategory spanned by those functors
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to S^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})
such that
X_{0}
is constant, i.e. the map
X_{0,\underline{0^{n-1}}}\to X_{0,\underline{t}}
is an equivalence for any
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

For any
n\geq 0
there is an equivalence between
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
and the
\infty
-category of
n
-fold Segal objects in
\mathscr{A}
, and this equivalence commutes with their inclusion into
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

Since both
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
and
S^{n}(\mathscr{A})
are full sub-
\infty
-categories of
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
, it will be enough to show that
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
admits the same inductive definition as
S^{n}(\mathscr{A})
. The cases
n=0
and
n=1
are clear. For
n\geq 2
, an
X\in\mathrm{Seg}(\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n-1,n-1}(\mathscr{A}))\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
has the property that the
(n-1)
-uple Segal object
X_{1}
is globular at height
k
for
1\leq k\leq n-1
, which holds if and only if
X
is globular at height
k
for
2\leq k\leq n
. By definition,
X_{0}
is constant if and only if
X
is globular at height
1
. Hence
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
if and only if, as a Segal functor
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n-1,n-1}(\mathscr{A})
, the value
X_{0}
is constant.
∎

We choose to avoid using the term “
n
-fold Segal space” in the rest of the paper, despite it being established in the literature. This is mostly because it can be easily confused with “
n
-uple Segal space” and because we feel that the adjective “globular” is a better descriptor for the condition satisfied by
n
-fold Segal spaces.

2.3.
Underlying globular objects

In
[
Hau18
, Proposition 4.12]
it is shown that the inclusion of
n
-fold Segal objects into
n
-uple Segal objects
1
1

1

A word of warning when checking the reference: Haugseng uses the notation
\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
for
n
-uple Segal objects and
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
for
n
-fold Segal objects, while we use
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
for the former and
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})
for the latter.
admits a right adjoint
U^{n}
. Therefore, given
2.9
, the inclusion
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
also admits a right adjoint. In this subsection we will produce a chain

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,2}(\mathscr{A})\to\dotsb\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})

of right adjoints to the inclusions
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}
whose composite is the right adjoint of the inclusion
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
; by
2.9
, the latter is equivalent to the right adjoint
U^{n}
of
[
Hau18
]
.

Proposition 2.10
.

For every
n\geq 1
and
1\leq k\leq n
, the inclusion
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})
has a right adjoint
R_{k}^{n}:\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

We concentrate first on the case
k=1
. The inclusion
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
corresponds to that of those functors
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})
whose value at
0
is constant, meaning we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Glb}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mapsto X_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mapsto X_{0,\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}

The top map has a right adjoint
R_{1}^{n}
by
[
Hau18
, Lemma 4.14]
: indeed, the bottom map admits a right adjoint (
2.4
), the right vertical map is a cartesian fibration by a slight modification of
[
Hau18
, Lemma 4.15]
, and the diagram is a pullback.

By induction on
n
and
k
we can assume that the inclusion
\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A})
admits a right adjoint
R_{k-1}^{n-1}
. The induced map
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-1}(\mathscr{A}))\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A}))
admits a right adjoint
P
sending
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A})
to
R_{k-1}^{n-1}(X_{\bullet}):\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-1}(\mathscr{A})
(
[
Hau18
, Lemma 4.18]
). Thanks to
2.4
, to show that
P
restricts to a right adjoint

R_{k}^{n}:\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})

it will be enough to show that, for all
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A})
, if
X_{0}
is constant then
P(X)_{0}
is constant. But
P(X)_{0}\simeq R^{n-1}_{k-1}(X_{0})\simeq X_{0}
because
X_{0}
(being constant) is already globular up to height
k-1
, so
R^{n-1}_{k-1}
fixes it; therefore
P(X)_{0}
is constant and we’re done.
∎

Proposition 2.11
.

The right adjoints
R_{k}^{n}:\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
restrict to right adjoints
R_{k}^{n}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
to the inclusion
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

The case
k=1
holds for the same reasons as in
2.10
since the constant diagram map
\mathscr{A}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1},\mathscr{A})
factors through
\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})
and since the map
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{A})
evaluating at
[0]
on the
first
coordinate is a cartesian fibration (again by
[
Hau18
, Lemma 4.15]
). Assume by induction that
R_{k-1}^{n-1}(X)
satisfies the Segal condition whenever
X
does. Then, if
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A}))
is written as a Segal presheaf
Y:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n-1,k-2}(\mathscr{A})
, we have that
R_{k}^{n}(X)_{s,\underline{t}}\simeq R_{k-1}^{n-1}(Y_{s})_{\underline{t}}
; thus
R_{k}^{n}(X)
satisfies the Segal condition in the first coordinate since
R_{k-1}^{n-1}
preserves all limits and in the remaining coordinates by the induction hypothesis. Hence
R_{k}^{n}
restricts to a functor
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
and the claim follows by
2.4
.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

The composite

R^{n}:=R_{n}^{n}R_{n-1}^{n}\dotsm R_{2}^{n}R_{1}^{n}:\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,1}(\mathscr{A})\to\dotsb\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})

is a right adjoint to the inclusion
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathscr{A})\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})
, and it is equivalent to the right adjoint
U^{n}
under the identification of
2.9
.

We also have a relatively explicit formula for
R_{k}^{n}(X)
in terms of
X
generalizing
[
Hau18
, Remark 4.13]
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\tau_{k}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{k,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
denote the functor which evaluates at
[0]
in the last
n-k
coordinates:
(\tau_{k}X)_{\underline{t}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},\underline{0}}
for any
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{k}
. We call
\tau_{k}X
the
truncation of
X
to height
k
.

Remark 2.14
.

Notice that
\tau_{k}
restricts to truncations functors

\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{k}(\mathscr{A}),\quad\mathrm{Glb}^{n,l}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Glb}^{k,l^{\prime}}(\mathscr{A}),\quad\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,l}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{k,l^{\prime}}(\mathscr{A})

where
l^{\prime}=l
if
l\leq n-k
and
l^{\prime}=k
if
l\geq n-k
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Fix
2\leq k\leq n+1
and let
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathscr{A})
. Denote by
e:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
the functor evaluating the
k
th coordinate at
[0]
, and let
r
be its right adjoint. Then the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{k}^{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\tau_{k-1}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(eX)}

in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{A})
is a pullback.

Proof.

First we will show that
P:=X\times_{r(eX)}r(\tau_{k-1}X)\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
by showing that
P_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\simeq P_{\underline{u},\underline{0^{n-k}}}
for any
\underline{u}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{k-1}
and
\underline{v}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-k-1}
. A quick computation shows that

(r(\tau_{k-1}X))_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\simeq X_{\underline{u},\underline{0^{n-k}}},\qquad(r(eX))_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\simeq X_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}

and so

P_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\simeq X_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\times_{X_{\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}X_{\underline{u},\underline{0^{n-k}}}\simeq X_{\underline{u},\underline{0^{n-k}}}\simeq P_{\underline{u},\underline{0^{n-k}}}.

Now note that if
Y\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
then

\mathrm{Map}(Y,r(\tau_{k-1}X))\simeq\mathrm{Map}(eY,\tau_{k-1}X)\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\tau_{k-1}Y,\tau_{k-1}X)

and

\mathrm{Map}(Y,r(eX))\simeq\mathrm{Map}(eY,eX)\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\tau_{k-1}Y,eX)\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\tau_{k-1}Y,\tau_{k-1}X).

since
eY\simeq\tau_{k-1}Y
by the globularity of
Y
at height
k
. The equivalences are seen to commute with the map
r(\tau_{k-1}X)\to r(eX)
, so

\mathrm{Map}(Y,P)\simeq\mathrm{Map}(Y,X)\times_{\mathrm{Map}(Y,r(eX))}\mathrm{Map}(Y,r(\tau_{k-1}X))\simeq\mathrm{Map}(Y,X)

naturally in
Y\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(\mathscr{A})
, which implies that
P\simeq R_{k}^{n}(X)
.
∎

2.4.
Segalification

From now on we will focus on the case
\mathscr{A}=\mathfrak{S}
which is central to the aim of this paper; nevertheless, most of what will appear can be generalized to the case of an arbitrary
\infty
-topos.

Recall that since
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S})
is obtained from the presheaf
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
by localizing at the Segal maps,
\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S})
is a
reflective sub-
\infty
-category
of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
, meaning that there is a left adjoint
\mathbb{L}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S})
to the inclusion. We call
\mathbb{L}
the
Segalification functor
. The inclusion
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
is also reflective and so comes with its own Segalification functor
\mathbb{L}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Definition 2.16
.

The
Segalification functor in the
k
th coordinate
is the functor
\mathbb{L}_{k}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S}))
defined by

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S}))\xlongrightarrow{\mathbb{L}\circ-}\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})

where the first equivalence moves the
k
th copy of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
to the right-hand side of
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
.

Note that we can apply
\mathbb{L}_{k}
iteratively, one
k
at a time, to obtain a functor
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
. This functor is
not equivalent
to
\mathbb{L}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
. Indeed, for any
k\neq l
,
\mathbb{L}_{l}\mathbb{L}_{k}X
is Segal in the
l
th coordinate but is not guaranteed to be Segal in the
k
th coordinate – this is essentially because
\mathbb{L}_{l}
might not preserve finite limits. There is a partial fix for this:

Fact 2.17

(
[
BS24
, Lemma 2.15]
)
.

Assume that
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
satisfies the Segal condition in the first
k-1
coordinates. Then
\mathbb{L}_{k}X
remains globular (i.e.
\mathbb{L}_{k}X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
) and satisfies the Segal condition in the first
k
coordinates.

Thus if we Segalify a globular presheaf one coordinate at a time we don’t have to worry about the Segal condition breaking at any point. The fix can be strengthened to presheaves that are globular only up to height
k
; in fact, the following result was the main drive to develop the definition of
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k}(-)
in the first place.

Proposition 2.18
.

Assume
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathfrak{S})
satisfies the Segal condition in the first
k-1
coordinates. Then
\mathbb{L}_{k}X
remains in
\mathrm{Glb}^{n,k-1}(\mathfrak{S})
and satisfies the Segal condition in the first
k
coordinates.

An immediate consequence is:

Corollary 2.19
.

If
X\in\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
then

(R_{n}^{n}\circ\mathbb{L}_{n}\circ R_{n-1}^{n}\circ\mathbb{L}_{n-1}\circ\dotsb\circ R_{1}^{n}\circ\mathbb{L}_{1})(X)

is a globular
n
-uple Segal space.

The proof of
2.18
is a straightforward modification of the proof of
[
BS24
, Lemma 2.15]
; we will write it down anyway, both for ease of reference and because we will make use of the intermediary
2.21
in later parts of the paper.

Definition 2.20
.

The
wedge

A\vee B
of two bipointed categories
(A,a_{0},a_{1})
and
(B,b_{0},b_{1})
is the bipointed category
(A\sqcup_{a_{1}=b_{0}}B,a_{0},b_{1})
. A
necklace
is an iterated wedge
N=[n_{1}]\vee[n_{2}]\vee\dotsb\vee[n_{k}]
where each representable
[n]
carries the bipointing
(0,n)
. We denote by
\mathbf{N}
the category whose objects are necklaces and whose morphisms are maps of bipointed categories.

Fact 2.21

(
[
BS24
, Observation 1.16, Proposition 1.17]
)
.

The Segalification functor
\mathbb{L}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S})
can be computed pointwise as

(\mathbb{L}X)_{t}\simeq\colim_{(N_{1},\dotsc,N_{t})\in\mathbf{N}^{n,\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}(N_{1}\vee N_{2}\vee\dotsb\vee N_{t},X).

In particular,
\mathbb{L}(X)_{0}\simeq X_{0}
and the space of
1
-morphisms of
\mathbb{L}X
is

(\mathbb{L}X)_{1}\simeq\colim_{N\in\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}(N,X)\simeq\colim_{[n_{1}]\vee\dotsb\vee[n_{k}]}X_{n_{1}}\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}X_{n_{k}}

and so any
1
-morphism of
\mathbb{L}X
can be written (possibly non-uniquely) as a formal composition of points of
X_{1}
.

Fact 2.22

(
[
BS24
, Lemmas 1.27, 2.14]
)
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Seg}(\mathfrak{S})
is a functor such that
F(-)_{0}:\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathfrak{S}
is constant. Then
\displaystyle\colim_{N\in\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}}F(N)
is a Segal space.

Proof of
2.18
.

If
i\leq k-2
and
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{i}
then
(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{k-i-1}(X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}})
; but
X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}}
is a constant
(n-i-1)
-simplicial space (since
X
is globular up to height
k-1
) and hence an
(n-i-1)
-uple Segal space, so it is fixed under Segalification. Therefore
(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{\bullet}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}\simeq(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}
and so
\mathbb{L}_{k}X
is also globular up to height
k-1
.

By construction
\mathbb{L}_{k}X
is Segal in the
k
th coordinate. To prove it remains Segal in the
i
th coordinate, for
i<k
, we just have to show that
(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},l,\underline{v}}
is a Segal space when
l=0,1
, where we have fixed
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{i-1}
,
[\underline{u}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{k-i-1}
, and
[\underline{v}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-k}
. For
l=0
there is nothing to do:
\mathbb{L}_{k}
fixes the space of objects (
2.21
) and so
(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}
is clearly Segal. For
l=1
,
2.21
gives us

(\mathbb{L}_{k}X)_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},1,\underline{v}}\simeq\colim_{[n_{1}]\vee\dotsb\vee[n_{j}]\in\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}}X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},n_{1},\underline{v}}\times_{X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}\dotsb\times_{X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},n_{j},\underline{v}}

as presheaves on
\mathbf{\Delta}
. We can now apply
2.22
: each
X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},l,\underline{v}}
is Segal by assumption (as
i<k
) and the Segal condition is preserved under pullbacks, so each
X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},n_{1},\underline{v}}\times_{X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}\dotsb\times_{X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}X_{\underline{t},\bullet,\underline{u},n_{j},\underline{v}}
is a Segal space; moreover, plugging
\bullet=0
yields

X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{u},n_{1},\underline{v}}\times_{X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}\dotsb\times_{X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{u},0,\underline{v}}}X_{\underline{t},0,\underline{u},n_{j},\underline{v}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}\times_{X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}}\dotsb\times_{X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}}X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}\simeq X_{\underline{t},\underline{0^{n-i}}}

since
X
is globular at height
i
for
i\leq k-1
. The fact implies that the colimit is again a Segal space, so we’re done.
∎

2.5.
A brief comment on completeness

There is an additional condition that one can impose on globular
n
-uple Segal spaces, called
completeness
, which we won’t dwell much on. They key property we need is that the full sub-
\infty
-category of
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
spanned by the complete objects is a model of the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,n)
-categories; this was proven for
n=1
in
[
JT07
]
and for arbitrary
n
in
[
Bar05
]
. In other words, we have a fully faithful functor
\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
with a left adjoint
C:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
called the
completion functor
. This is good news for us since the
(\infty,n)
-category we are interested in should have a mapping-out universal property, so we can reasonably construct it as
C(X)
for some
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
. Then, instead of studying
C(X)
closely, we can study the space
\mathrm{Map}(C(X),\mathscr{D})
of functors into an
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathscr{D}
by studying the (possibly simpler) equivalent space
\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{D})
.

In light of this equivalence, from now on we will use the term “
(\infty,n)
-category” to mean “complete globular
n
-uple Segal space”. We will also treat every strict
n
-category as an
(\infty,n)
-category and thus as a globular
n
-uple Segal space.

Remark 2.23
.

There is a functor
w:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{n}
which sends
[\underline{t}]
to the
walking
\underline{t}
-cell
. It is uniquely determined by the following three properties:

(a)

w(k):=w([1,\dotsc,1,0,\dotsc,0])
is the walking
k
-morphism;

(b)

if
\underline{t}=(\underline{r},0,\underline{s})
then
w(\underline{t})=w(\underline{r},\underline{0})
;

(c)

w
is co-Segal: it sends the Segal maps to pushouts.

It can be verified that the composite
w:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
satisfies

\mathrm{Map}(w(\underline{t}),X)\simeq\mathrm{Map}([\underline{t}],X)\simeq X_{\underline{t}}

for any globular
n
-uple Segal space
X
.

2.6.
Adjoint morphisms

We conclude this section with a few definitions.

Definition 2.24
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Adj}
the universal
2
-category containing an adjunction
f\dashv g
(see, for example,
[
RV16
]
). Explicitly,
\mathrm{Adj}
is generated by two objects
x,y
, two
1
-morphisms
f:x\to y
,
g:y\to x
, and two
2
-morphism
\eta:\mathrm{id}(x)\Rightarrow gf
,
\varepsilon:fg\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}(y)
satisfying the snake equations

(\varepsilon\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}(f))\circ_{2}(\mathrm{id}(f)\circ_{1}\eta)=\mathrm{id}(f),\qquad(\mathrm{id}(g)\circ_{1}\varepsilon)\circ_{2}(\eta\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}(g))=\mathrm{id}(g).

As usual, we call
f
the
left adjoint
,
g
the
right adjoint
,
\eta
the
unit
, and
\varepsilon
the
counit
. An
adjunction of
1
-morphisms
in an
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathscr{C}
is a map
\mathrm{Adj}\to\mathscr{C}
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
(\infty,n)
-category and let
1\leq k\leq n-1
. An
adjunction of
k
-morphisms
in
\mathscr{C}
is an adjunction of
1
-morphisms in
\mathrm{Mor}^{k-1}(\mathscr{C})
, the
(\infty,n-k+1)
-category of
(k-1)
-morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
. If
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{nn,}(\mathfrak{S})
has completion
\mathscr{X}
, an
adjunction of
k
-morphisms
in
X
is an adjunction of
k
-morphisms in
\mathscr{X}
.

Remark 2.26
.

If
X
is not complete we can still compute the adjunctions in it without first completing it: they are given as in
2.24
except that the snake equations only have to hold up to an invertible higher morphism. If
X
is complete then higher invertible morphisms are equivalent to identities, so the problem of distinguishing the two definitions does not arise.

We borrow the following terminology from
[
DPP03
]
:

Definition 2.27
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
. We say that
X
is
k-sinister
if every
k
-morphism of
X
extends to an adjunction of
k
-morphisms in
X
for which it is a left adjoint. We say that
X
is
sinister
if it is
k
-sinister for all
k=1,\dotsc,n-1
.

3.
Squares and zigzags

In this section we will define our main object of interest, a functor
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n+1,n+1}(\mathfrak{S})
such that the morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
are certain zigzag diagrams in
X
. First we will have to introduce an auxiliary functor
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
, the
higher square functor
, which we then modify a bit to obtain
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}
.

3.1.
Lax cubes

We borrow the following result from
[
Cam23
, Theorem A]
:

Proposition 3.1
.

For any
n\geq 1
there is a functor
-\otimes-:\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\times\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
which turns
\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
into a closed monoidal
\infty
-category with unit
[0]
, the terminal
(\infty,n)
-category.

Definition 3.2
.

Denote by
\square^{n}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
the composite

y\otimes\dotsb\otimes y:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\hookrightarrow(\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})^{n}\hookrightarrow(\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)})^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}

of the
n
-fold product of the Yoneda embedding
y
with the tensor product
\otimes
from Campion’s theorem. We call
\square(n):=\square^{n}(1,\dotsc,1)\in\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
the
n
-dimensional lax cube
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The functor
\square^{n}
has the following properties:

(1)

it factors through gaunt strict
n
-categories;

(2)

it is co-Segal: its values are given by iterated pushouts of the values of elementary
n
-tuples, and these pushouts can be computed strictly or weakly;

(3)

for any
a,b\geq 0
such that
a+b=n+1
there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{a}\times\{[0]\}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{n}}

Proof.

The first property follows from the fact that
\otimes
restricts to the usual Gray tensor product of strict
n
-categories. The second property holds since the Yoneda embedding
y:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
is co-Segal and
\otimes
preserves colimits in each variable; that the pushouts can be computed strictly is spelled out in detail in
[
Cam23
]
. The third property is a reformulation of the fact that
[0]
is the unit of
\otimes
.
∎

Our next order of business is to understand
\square(n)
for all
n
; thanks to the two properties above this is enough to understand
\square^{n}(\underline{t})
for all
\underline{t}
. The description of
\square(n)
that we will use is a small modification of the one presented in
[
AABS02
]
with the only difference occurring in the sign rule that determines the orientation of the boundary of a given
k
-morphism of
\square(n)
. This will be made clearer once we have defined the objects of interest and stated the characterization.

We fix a combinatorial
n
-cube
I^{n}
, the
n
-fold product of the combinatorial interval
I=\{0,1\}
. Let
p^{0}=\{0\}
and
p^{1}=\{1\}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A
k
-dimensional face
f
of
I^{n}
is a word
a_{1}a_{2}\dotsb a_{n}
of length
n
in the alphabet
\{p^{0},p^{1},I\}
containing exactly
k
many copies of
I
. For two faces
f=a_{1}a_{2}\dotsb a_{n}
and
g=b_{1}b_{2}\dotsb b_{n}
, we say that
f
is contained in
g
and write
f\subseteq g
if
a_{i}\neq b_{i}
implies
b_{i}=I
.

Remark 3.5
.

The characteristic map of a face
f
is the injective function
\chi_{f}:I^{k}\to I^{n}
defined as the product
\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}\times\dotsb\times\varphi_{n}
, where

\varphi_{i}=\begin{cases}p^{l}\hookrightarrow I&\text{if }a_{i}=p^{l},\\
\mathrm{id}_{I}&\text{if }a_{i}=I\end{cases}

is either an inclusion or the identity map on
I
. Geometrically, a face
f=a_{1}a_{2}\dotsm a_{n}
with
a_{i}\in\{p^{0},p^{1},I\}
corresponds to the subset
\prod_{i}a_{i}\subseteq I^{n}
, which is also the image of
\chi_{f}
.

Definition 3.6
.

We define the
parity
\sigma(f,g)
of
f
relative to
g
for a
k
-dimensional face
f
contained in a
(k+1)
-dimensional face
g
of
I^{n}
.

•

If
k=n-1
then
g=I^{n}
and
f
contains exactly one copy of
p^{l}
, say in coordinate
r
, for some
l\in\{0,1\}
. The parity of
f
is the number
\sigma(f,I^{n}):=r+l+n
computed modulo
2
.

•

If
k<n-1
then the characteristic map of
f
factors through that of
g
via a function
I^{k}\to I^{k+1}
. In this case we set
n=k+1
and treat
g
as
I^{n}
,
f
as a face of
g
of dimension
n-1=k
, and apply the previous definition.

Remark 3.7
.

An equivalent way to compute
\sigma(f,g)
is as follows. Take the word
g=b_{1}\dotsb b_{n}
and erase all
n-k-1
copies of
p^{l}
, leaving only the
k+1
copies of
I
. Say that those copies of
p^{l}
were
b_{i_{1}},\dotsc,b_{i_{n-k-1}}
. Now erase from
f=a_{1}\dotsb a_{n}
the entries
a_{i_{1}},\dotsc,a_{i_{n-k-1}}
, which were all copies of
p^{l}
since
f\subseteq g
. Now the resulting word has length
k+1
and contains
k
copies of
I
and a single
p^{l}
, say in the new coordinate
r
. The number
\sigma(f,g)
is precisely
r+l+(k+1)
.

Fact 3.8

(Reformulation of
[
AABS02
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

For any
n
,
\square(n)
is a strict gaunt finite
n
-category with the following properties:

(a)

if
G^{n}_{k}
denotes the set of
k
-dimensional faces of
I^{n}
then there is an injection from
G^{n}_{k}\hookrightarrow M^{n}_{k}
to the set of non-degenerate
k
-morphisms of
\square(n)
;

(b)

if
f\in G^{n}_{k}
, the source/target of
f
when treated as a
k
-morphism of
\square(n)
is the (unique) composition of the even/odd
(k-1)
-dimensional faces of
f
, where the parity is calculated relative to
f
;

(c)

the subset
G^{n}_{k}
and the identity
k
-morphisms of lower dimensional morphisms freely generate
M^{n}_{k}
under composition, where freely means that the only relation is the cancellation of identities.

Remark 3.9
.

As mentioned before, the only change we made from the construction in
[
AABS02
]
is in how the parity of a face is defined; translating the definition given right before their Theorem 1.3 we see that they set it to be
\sigma(f,I^{n})=r+l+1
, without the contribution of the dimension
n
. Thus our parity corresponds with theirs when
n
is odd and is opposite to theirs when
n
is even. The parity only changes the direction of the even-dimensional morphisms, so one can pass from one model to the other by applying
(-)^{\mathrm{op}_{2},\mathrm{op}_{4},\dotsc}
. We will explain later why our definition works better for our purposes.

Example 3.10
.

Here are the first three values of
\square(n)
. These can be computed from
3.8
or looked up – for example, they appear in
[
Cam23
, Figure 1]
.

(0)

\square(0)
is the terminal category
[0]
, which we draw like this:
\bullet
.

(1)

\square(1)
is the walking arrow
[1]
, which we draw like this:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
.

(2)

\square(2)
is the walking lax commutative square, which we draw like this:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

\Downarrow

.

(3)

We draw
\square(3)
as two
2
-categories connected by a
3
-morphism:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
F(n)
denote the poset of
k
-dimensional faces of
I^{n}
for
0\leq k<n
, ordered by containment. Then there is a functor
F(n)\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
,
f\mapsto\square(\dim f)
whose colimit
\partial\square(n)\in\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
is the underlying sub-
(n-1)
-category of
\square(n)
. In particular we have a colimit decomposition

\square(n)\simeq\partial\square(n)\sqcup_{w(n-1)\sqcup w(n-1)}w(n)

where
w(n)
is the walking
n
-morphism.

Proof.

This is immediate from the fact that the
k
-dimensional faces of
I^{n}
generate all the
k
-morphisms of
\square(n)
, and that the compositions are all free (i.e. obtained by pasting walking
k
-morphisms together).
∎

3.2.
Some maps of lax cubes

The advantage of having an explicit description of
\square(n)
is that mapping out of it becomes easier. In this subsection we will study “collapse” maps
\kappa(n):\square(n)\to w(n)
onto the walking
n
-morphism.

First recall that
w(n)
has exactly two non-degenerate parallel
k
-morphisms for
0\leq k<n
and a single
n
-morphism. Label the unique
n
-morphism by
J^{n}
and label the two
k
-morphisms via the words
J^{k}q^{l}
; with respect to the (one or two)
(k+1)
-morphism(s) in
w(n)
, the one with
l=0
is the source and the one with
l=1
is the target. Note that the source and target of
J^{k}q^{l}
are given by
J^{k-1}q^{0}
and
J^{k-1}q^{1}
, respectively. Now, for a face
f
of
I^{n}
of dimension smaller than
n
there exists a unique pair
(k_{f},l_{f})
such that the word representing
f
begins with
I^{k_{f}}p^{l_{f}}
. Note that
\dim f\geq k_{f}
since there could be some more copies of
I
in the word representing
f
that appear after
p^{l_{f}}
.

Definition 3.12
.

Define
\kappa(n)
on a generator
f
of
\square(n)
by

\kappa(n)(f):=\mathrm{id}^{\dim f-k_{f}}(J^{k_{f}}q^{l_{f}})

if
\dim f<n
and
\kappa(n)(I^{n}):=J^{n}
. Composition in
\square(n)
is free so we are forced to define
\kappa(n)(f\circ_{k}g):=\kappa(n)(f)\circ_{k}\kappa(n)(g)
for arbitrary morphisms
f,g
of
\square(n)
.

Example 3.13
.

It might be instructive to draw what happens when
n=2
and
n=3
(
\kappa(n)
is the identity when
n\leq 1
). This is
\kappa(2)
:

Obviously, in both examples, whenever a
k
-morphism
f
on the left has the same color as a
j
-morphism
g
on the right and
j<k
then we are indicating that
f
is sent to the corresponding identity
k
-morphism of
g
.

Proposition 3.14
.

The associations above make
\kappa(n)
into a functor
\square(n)\to w(n)
of
n
-categories.

Proof.

Composition is taken care of, as are identities, so we just have to check that
\kappa(n)
preserves the sources and targets of the generating morphisms. We will only treat the case where
\dim f=k_{f}
, as the general case can be deduced from it. If
\dim f=k_{f}
then
f
starts with
I^{k_{f}}p^{l_{f}}
and contains no other copies of
I
. Then
\kappa(n)(f)=J^{k_{f}}q^{l_{f}}
, so we have to verify that the source of
f
is sent to
J^{k_{f}-1}q^{0}
. Now, the even subfaces of
f
come in two flavors: there’s the one which starts with
I^{k_{f}-1}p^{0}p^{l_{f}}
, which
\kappa(n)
sends to
J^{k_{f}-1}q^{0}
, and the other ones all start with
I^{a}p^{c}I^{b}p^{l_{f}}
, which forces
\kappa(n)
to send them to some identity of a lower dimensional morphism. Therefore the composition of the even subfaces of
f
, i.e. the source of
f
, is sent to a composition of
J^{k_{f}-1}q^{0}
with some identities of lower dimensional morphisms; but the only composition of that form available in
w(n)
returns
J^{k_{f}-1}q^{0}
, which is what we needed. The same argument applied to
I^{k_{f}-1}p^{1}p^{l_{f}}
shows that the target of
f
is sent to
J^{k_{f}-1}q^{1}
.
∎

We want to extend
\kappa(n)
to a natural transformation of functors
\square^{n}\to w
. To do this we will need the following technical lemma:

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
F,G:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{n}
be Segal functors valued in strict
n
-categories; in particular, the Segal maps are isomorphisms, not just equivalences. Let
\sigma,\tau:[0]\to[1]
and
\rho:[1]\to[0]
be the three maps in
\mathbf{\Delta}
between
[0]
and
[1]
, and let
\gamma:[1]\to[2]
denote the unique active map. Let
\{\alpha_{\underline{t}}:F(\underline{t})\to G(\underline{t})\mid\underline{t}\text{ is an elementary $n$-tuple}\}
be a collection of maps defined for elementary
n
-tuples. Extend
\alpha_{\underline{t}}:F(\underline{t})\to G(\underline{t})
to non-elementary
n
-tuples via the Segal maps:

\alpha_{\underline{t}}:=\colim_{\underline{e}\to\underline{t}}\alpha_{\underline{e}}:F(\underline{t})\cong\colim_{\underline{e}\to\underline{t}}F(\underline{e})\to\colim_{\underline{e}\to\underline{t}}G(\underline{e})\cong G(\underline{t})

where the colimit is taken over all inert map
\underline{e}\to\underline{t}
with
\underline{e}
elementary. Then
\alpha_{\underline{t}}
for
\underline{t}\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
form the components of a natural transformation
\alpha:F\to G
if and only if the natural diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\underline{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\underline{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\underline{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\underline{t})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\underline{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\underline{t}}}

commutes for all
\underline{s},\underline{t},\varphi
with the following properties:

(1)

\underline{s}=(\underline{a},s_{i},\underline{b})
and
\underline{t}=(\underline{a},t_{i},\underline{b})
with
\underline{a}
and
\underline{b}
both elementary tuples;

(2)

\varphi_{i}:[s_{i}]\to[t_{i}]
is one of
\sigma
,
\tau
,
\rho
, or
\gamma
;

(3)

\varphi_{j}=\mathrm{id}
for all
j\neq i
.

Proof.

One direction is clear. For the other, we need to show that all naturality diagrams commute. It’s enough to check this for face and degeneracy maps, as they generate
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
under composition. A face map is the product of identities together with a map
[k]\to[k+1]
whose image misses the value
i
; if
i=0
we can write this map as

[k-1]\cong[0]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-1]\xlongrightarrow{\tau\sqcup\mathrm{id}}[1]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-1]\cong[k],

if
i=k+1
we can write it as

[k-1]\cong[k-1]\sqcup_{[0]}[0]\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\sqcup\sigma}[k-1]\sqcup_{[0]}[1]\cong[k],

and if
1\leq i\leq k
we can write it as

[k-1]\cong[i]\sqcup_{[0]}[1]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-i-2]\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\sqcup\gamma\sqcup\mathrm{id}}[i]\sqcup_{[0]}[2]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-i-2]\cong[k].

Similarly, a degeneracy map is the product of identities together with a map of the form
[k+1]\to[k]
with some
i
in the codomain having exactly two preimages; we can always write the last map as

[k]\cong[i]\sqcup_{[0]}[1]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-i-1]\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\sqcup\rho\sqcup\mathrm{id}}[i]\sqcup_{[0]}[0]\sqcup_{[0]}[k-i-1]\cong[k-1].

Thus we can observe that the naturality diagram of a face or degeneracy map is a colimit of naturality diagrams of maps which are products of identities and one of
\sigma,\tau,\rho,\gamma
. If the latter all commute, which is the assumption in the claim, then all their colimits commute; thus all naturality diagrams commute, concluding the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.16
.

There is a natural transformation
\kappa:\square\to w
of functors
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}
such that the component of
\kappa
at
(1,\dotsc,1)
is given by
\kappa(n)
.

Proof.

First we define
\kappa_{\underline{t}}
on elementary
n
-tuples
\underline{t}
as follows: if
\underline{t}=(\underline{1},0,\underline{s})
with
\underline{s}
an elementary
(n-k)
-tuple (so there are
k-1
many
1
s at the start of
\underline{t}
), then

\kappa_{\underline{t}}:\square(\underline{t})\to\square(\underline{1},\underline{0})\simeq\square(k-1)\xlongrightarrow{\kappa(k-1)}w(k-1)\simeq w(\underline{1},\underline{0})\simeq w(\underline{t})

where the first map is induced by the projection
\underline{s}\to\underline{0}
, the second is the equivalence of
3.3
, and the last two are the equivalences in
2.23
. Now we can define
\kappa_{\underline{t}}
for non-elementary
\underline{t}
by extending along the Segal decomposition of
\square(\underline{t})
and
w(\underline{t})
. Using the lemma, to prove that
\kappa_{\underline{t}}
is natural in
\underline{t}
it will be enough to prove that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{n}(\underline{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(\underline{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{n}(\underline{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(\underline{t})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\underline{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square^{n}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\underline{t}}}

commutes for all
\underline{s},\underline{t},\varphi
with the following properties:

(1)

\underline{s}=(\underline{a},s_{i},\underline{b})
and
\underline{t}=(\underline{a},t_{i},\underline{b})
with
\underline{a}
and
\underline{b}
both elementary tuples;

(2)

\varphi_{i}:[s_{i}]\to[t_{i}]
is one of
\sigma
,
\tau
,
\rho
, or
\gamma
;

(3)

\varphi_{j}=\mathrm{id}
for all
j\neq i
.

The cases
\varphi_{i}\in\{\sigma,\tau,\rho\}
follow immediately from the definition of
\kappa_{\underline{t}}
. For
\varphi_{i}=\gamma
,
\underline{s}=(\underline{a},1,\underline{b})
, and
\underline{t}=(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})
we have two cases

(1)

If
\underline{a}
starts with
k
many
1
s followed by at least one
0
then we can rewrite the diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(\underline{a},1,\underline{b})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(\underline{a},1,\underline{b})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
f
is the map

\square(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})\cong\square(\underline{a},1,\underline{b})\sqcup_{\square(\underline{a},0,\underline{b})}\square(\underline{a},1,\underline{b})\to\square(k)\sqcup_{\square(k)}\square(k)\simeq\square(k).

This ends up being equivalent to the projection
\square(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})\to\square(k)
induced by the map
(\underline{a},2,\underline{b})\to(\underline{1},0,\dotsc,0)
, so the left square commutes. The other two obviously commute as well, and so the original diagram commutes.

(2)

If
\underline{a}=\underline{1}
then we may as well assume
\underline{b}=\underline{1}
, since the case where
\underline{b}
contains a
0
can be turned into a lower dimensional case where
\underline{b}
does not contain any
0
s. Assume the
2
appears in coordinate
k+1
. Then the diagram reads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(n)\sqcup_{\square(\underline{1},0,\underline{1})}\square(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square(n)\sqcup_{\square(k)}\square(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(n)\sqcup_{w(k)}w(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(n)\sqcup_{\kappa(k)}\kappa(n)}

That this diagram commutes can be verified on the generators of
\square(n)
and it’s mainly just an exercise in understanding the notation, so we leave it to the reader. ∎

Example 3.17
.

To see how
\kappa
is defined on elementary representables in low dimensional cases we refer the reader to
3.13
. There, the left red face is sent to the left red vertex of
w(3)
, and the map doing that is precisely
\kappa_{0,1,1}:\kappa(0,1,1)\to w(0,1,1)\cong w(0)
; similarly for the right olive face. The front face is collapsed to an edge via the map
\kappa_{1,0,1}
, and similarly for the back face, while the top and bottom faces are sent to copies of
w(2)
via
\kappa_{1,1,0}
.

3.3.
Higher square functor

Definition 3.18
.

The
higher square functor

\mathrm{Sq}^{n}:\mathrm{Glb}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
is defined by currying the functor

\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\xlongrightarrow{(\square^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{id}}\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\subseteq\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Map}(-,-)}\mathfrak{S}

to a functor
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
, which ends up landing in
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Remark 3.19
.

For any
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
the
\underline{t}
-morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)
satisfy

\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)_{\underline{t}}\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\square^{n}(\underline{t}),X).

Thus, given the description of
3.8
, we can think of points of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)_{\underline{t}}
as certain iterated cubical diagrams in
X
.

Example 3.20
.

In the case
n=2
the
(a,b)
-morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
are easy to draw in terms of the morphisms of
X
: they are diagrams in
X
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

\Downarrow

\Downarrow

\Downarrow

\Downarrow

\Downarrow

\Downarrow

with
a
columns and
b
rows of
2
-morphsims
\Rightarrow
, each one going between two composite
1
-morphisms. The composition of two diagrams is given by pasting them together and then composing the resulting
2
-morphisms.

Remark 3.21
.

Recall that there is a natural transformation
\kappa:\square^{n}\to w
(
3.16
). From the definition of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}
we see that
\kappa
induces a natural transformation of functors from the inclusion
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
to the higher square functor
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
, which we denote by
\kappa^{\ast}
. In particular we have a map
\kappa^{\ast}_{X}:X\to\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)
for any
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
which we call the
canonical inclusion
. Indeed, it can be shown that
\kappa_{X}^{\ast}
induces an equivalence
X\simeq U^{n}\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)
between
X
and the underlying fully globular
n
-uple Segal space of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}(X)
.

Example 3.22
.

In the case
n=2
, by examining
3.13
we see that the canonical inclusion sends a
2
-morphism
\alpha:w(2)\to X
of
X
to the
(1,1)
-morphism
\kappa^{2}_{X}(\alpha)\simeq\alpha\circ\kappa(2):\square^{2}\to w(2)\to X
of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
obtained by thickening
\alpha
in the vertical direction:
