{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -8.03 -11.68 L 7.47 -11.68}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.60002pt}{-8.4444pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

For arbitrary
n
the picture is very similar: an
n
-morphism is taken to a thickened version of itself that fits in a cubical mold.

Next we introduce
signatures
for the square functor.

Definition 3.23
.

Consider the walking span

\Lambda:\,\quad-\leftarrow 0\rightarrow+

and its iterated product
\Lambda^{n}
. An object of
\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{n}
, called a
signature
, is an
n
-tuple valued in
\{-,0,+\}
. For each symbol
s\in\{-,0,+\}
we define a functor
F_{s}:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
:

•

F_{+}
is the identity functor;

•

F_{-}
is the functor reversing the linear order of each element of
\mathbf{\Delta}
;

•

F_{0}
is the constant functor on
[0]
.

Thus for every signature
\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{n}
we get a functor
F_{\underline{b}}=\prod_{i}F_{b_{i}}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}\to\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
.

Definition 3.24
.

The
higher square functor with signature
\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{n}
, denoted
\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{\underline{b}}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
, is defined by currying the functor

\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\xlongrightarrow{((\square^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\circ F_{\underline{b}}^{\mathrm{op}})\times\mathrm{id}}\mathfrak{Cat}_{(\infty,n)}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\subseteq\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Map}(-,-)}\mathfrak{S}

to a functor
\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
which ends up landing in
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Remark 3.25
.

Explicitly, say that
\underline{b}
has a
0
in coordinates
z_{1}<z_{2}<\dotsb<z_{p}
and a
-
in coordinates
m_{1}<m_{2}<\dotsc<m_{q}
; then

\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{\underline{b}}(X)\simeq I_{z_{p}}\dotsm I_{z_{2}}I_{z_{1}}(\mathrm{Sq}^{n-p}(\tau_{n-p}X)^{\mathrm{op}_{m_{1}},\mathrm{op}_{m_{2}},\dotsc,\mathrm{op}_{m_{q}}})

where
I_{r}:\mathrm{Seg}^{k}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{k+1}
is the left adjoint of the evaluation functor at
[0]
on the
r
th factor of
\mathbf{\Delta}
. This follows from the fact that
\square^{n}(\underline{t},\underline{0})\simeq\square^{n-p}(\underline{t})
for any
(n-p)
-tuple
\underline{t}
. In particular, for any
\underline{c}=(\underline{a},\underline{b})\in\Lambda^{m}\times\Lambda^{n}
we have

\tau_{m}\mathrm{Sq}^{m+n}_{\underline{c}}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{m+n}_{\underline{a},\underline{0}}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{m}_{\underline{a}}(\tau_{m}X).

Remark 3.26
.

The assignment
\underline{b}\mapsto\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{\underline{b}}
is functorial over
\Lambda^{n}
: there are maps

\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{b_{1},\dotsc,0,\dotsc,b_{n}}(X)\to\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{b_{1},\dotsc,-,\dotsc,b_{n}}(X),\quad\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{b_{1},\dotsc,0,\dotsc,b_{n}}(X)\to\mathrm{Sq}^{n}_{b_{1},\dotsc,+,\dotsc,b_{n}}(X),

where we changed the
i
th coordinate, and these maps pairwise commute for different values of
i
.

3.4.
Zigzagification

Fix
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
. Since in particular
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n+1,n+1}(\mathfrak{S})
, for every
\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{n}
we can construct the
(n+1)
-uple Segal space
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},+}(X)
, naturally in
\underline{b}
and
X
. Let
Z^{n+1}_{+}(X)\in\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{n+1,\mathrm{op}},\mathfrak{S})
denote the presheaf obtained by taking the pointwise colimit of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},+}(X)
:

Z^{n+1}_{+}(X)_{\underline{t}}:=\colim_{\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{n}}\left(\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},+}(X)_{\underline{t}}\right).

Note that
Z^{n+1}_{+}(X)
is
not
an
(n+1)
-uple Segal space, since the colimit was taken only pointwise. At this point we could apply the Segalification functor
\mathbb{L}^{n+1}
from
Section

2.4
but doing so naively would not yield the desired result. Instead, consider the following:

Definition 3.27
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n}(\mathfrak{S})
. The
(positive) zigzagification of
X
is

\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X):=(R_{n+1}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{n+1}R_{n}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{n}\dotsb R_{1}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{1})(Z^{n+1}_{+}(X)),

which is a globular
(n+1)
-uple Segal space by
2.19
.

Remark 3.28
.

The canonical inclusion
\kappa_{X}^{\ast}:X\to\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
extends to a map
X\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
of globular
n
-uple Segal spaces: this is because
X
is already globular and Segal, and so applying
R_{i}^{n}
and
\mathbb{L}_{j}
does not affect it.

Remark 3.29
.

There is a
negative
zigzagification
\mathcal{Z}_{-}^{n+1}(X)
of
X
as well, obtained by replacing the
+
with a
-
in the definition of
Z^{n+1}_{+}(X)
. It can be readily seen that
Z^{n+1}_{-}(X)\simeq(Z^{n+1}_{+}(X))^{\mathrm{op}_{n+1}}
and so
\mathcal{Z}_{-}^{n+1}(X)\simeq\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)^{\mathrm{op}_{n+1}}
since segalification and globularization commute with taking opposites.

Proposition 3.30
.

For any
0\leq k\leq n
we have

\tau_{k}\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)\simeq(R_{k}^{k}\mathbb{L}_{k}\dotsb R_{1}^{k}\mathbb{L}_{1})(\tau_{k}Z^{n+1}_{+}(X))\simeq(R_{k}^{k}\mathbb{L}_{k}\dotsb R_{1}^{k}\mathbb{L}_{1})(Z^{k}(\tau_{k}X))

where
Z^{k}(Y)_{\underline{t}}\simeq\displaystyle\colim_{\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{k}}\left(\mathrm{Sq}^{k}_{\underline{b}}(Y)_{\underline{t}}\right)
for any
Y\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{k,k}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Proof.

The first equivalence is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbb{L}_{j}
and
R_{j}^{m}
commute with evaluation at
[0]
for any
j
and
m
. For the second equivalence, write

Z_{+}^{n+1}(X)_{\underline{t}}\simeq\colim_{\underline{c}\in\Lambda^{n-k-1}}\colim_{\underline{b}\in\Lambda^{k}}\left(\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},\underline{c},+}(X)\right)_{\underline{t}}.

Then applying
\tau_{k}
turns each
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},\underline{c},+}(X)
into
\mathrm{Sq}^{k}_{\underline{b},\underline{0}}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{k}_{\underline{b}}(\tau_{k}X)
by
3.25
and the outermost colimit becomes a constant colimit at
\mathrm{Sq}^{k}_{\underline{b}}(\tau_{k}X)
.
∎

Using the formula for
\mathbb{L}_{k}
given in
2.21
and the previous proposition we can describe
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
informally as follows:

•

its objects are the objects of
X
;

•

its
1
-morphisms are zigzags
1
-morphisms of
X
;

•

its
2
-morphisms are obtained by taking all lax commutative squares in
X
, forming globular zigzags in the first direction, and then forming zigzags of those in the second direction;

•

its
3
-morphisms are obtained by taking all lax commuative cubes in
X
, forming globular zigzags in the first direction, then forming globular zigzags of those in the second direction, and finally forming zigzags of those in the third direction;

•

its
k
-morphisms, in general, are iterated zigzags of lax commutative
k
-cubes in
X
where the zigzags are taken in order from the first direction to the
k
th direction.

4.
Free adjoints and zigzagification

In this section we will prove that the functor
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}:\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{1,1}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{2,2}(\mathfrak{S})
freely adds right adjoints to the morphisms of a Segal space. The main technical hurdle is to show that, in a technical sense,
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
is generated by the adjunction data. We first prove some helpful results about generating spaces in globular Segal spaces.

4.1.
Some results on generators

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
be a globular
n
-uple Segal space. Let
A\hookrightarrow X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
be a monomorphism. Recall that the Segal condition gives us composition operations
-\circ_{j}-
on
X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
for
1\leq j\leq n
. We say that
A

generates
X
under composition
if it has the following properties:

(1)

for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
, the iterated identity map
\mathrm{id}(-):X_{\underline{1^{k}},\underline{0^{n-k}}}\to X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
factors through
A
;

(2)

the smallest subspace of
X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
which contains
A
and is closed under all the composition operations is
X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
.

We call the
k
-morphisms
f\in A
the
generating
k
-morphisms
.

Remark 4.2
.

The first condition implies that
A
is closed under the source and target maps, as those land in the morphisms of dimension strictly lower than
n
. In particular, if
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{el}}
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}
spanned by the elementary
n
-tuples (recall that these are the ones whose entries are in
\{0,1\}
), then
A
determines a functor
A^{\mathrm{el}}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{el}}\to\mathfrak{S}
with
A^{\mathrm{el}}_{\underline{1^{n}}}\simeq A
and
A^{\mathrm{el}}_{\underline{e}}\simeq X_{\underline{e}}
for any other elementary
\underline{e}\neq\underline{1^{n}}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
. Any functor
W:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{el}}\to\mathfrak{S}
with a map to
W\to X|_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{el}}}
can be extended to a functor
M(W):\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}\to\mathfrak{S}
with a map
M(W)\to X
that returns the given map upon restricting to
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{el}}
. Indeed, for any
[\underline{t}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,\mathrm{op}}
we can define

M(W)_{\underline{t}}:=\left(\lim_{[\underline{e}]\to[\underline{t}]}W_{\underline{e}}\right)\times_{\left(\displaystyle\lim_{[\underline{e}]\to[\underline{t}]}X_{\underline{e}}\right)}X_{\underline{t}}\hookrightarrow X_{\underline{t}}

where the limits are taken over elementary
n
-tuples
\underline{e}
; it’s not hard to see that
M(W)_{\underline{t}}
is natural in
\underline{t}
and that the projections to
X_{\underline{t}}
define the induced map
M(W)\hookrightarrow X
. In the case
W=A^{\mathrm{el}}
,
M(A^{\mathrm{el}})
is the
n
-simplicial space whose value at
\underline{t}
is the subspace of
X_{\underline{t}}
all whose elementary components (the projections to
X_{\underline{e}}
) land in
A
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
and let
A\hookrightarrow X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
generate
X
under composition. Then there is a sequence of
n
-simplicial spaces

M(A^{\mathrm{el}})=M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})\hookrightarrow M(A(2)^{\mathrm{el}})\hookrightarrow M(A(3)^{\mathrm{el}})\hookrightarrow\dotsb

whose colimit is equivalent to
X
.

Proof.

Fix a
p\geq 1
and let
I_{p}
denote the category of inert maps
[\underline{e}]\to[\underline{p^{n}}]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n}
such that
\underline{e}
is an elementary
n
-tuple. Define the space

A(p):=\mathrm{im}\left(\lim_{([\underline{e}]\to[\underline{p^{n}}])\in I_{p}}A^{\mathrm{el}}_{\underline{e}}\to\lim_{([\underline{e}]\to[\underline{p^{n}}])\in I_{p}}X_{\underline{e}}\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}X_{\underline{p^{n}}}\xlongrightarrow{c}X_{\underline{1^{n}}}\right)\hookrightarrow X_{\underline{1^{n}}}

as the essential image of the total composition map
c:X_{\underline{p^{n}}}\to X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
(induced by the function
[1]\cong\{0,p\}\to[p]
in all coordinates) with its domain restricted so that the elementary components of each
f\in X_{\underline{p^{n}}}
come from
A
. Informally (but correctly), we think of
A(p)
as the subspace of
X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
containing those morphisms which can be obtained starting from
A
by doing at most
p
iterated compositions in each direction (so a total of
np
compositions). Note that since
A
generates
X
and
A\subseteq A(p)
for any
p
,
A(p)
also generates
X
. Using
4.2
and
4.3
we can extend
A(p)
to
M(A(p)^{\mathrm{el}})
. It’s clear that
A(1)\simeq A
and hence that
M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})\simeq M(A^{\mathrm{el}})
, and the induced maps come from the inclusions
A(p)\hookrightarrow A(p+1)
obtained, for example, by composing with identities at the last step in every direction.

Finally we need
X\simeq\displaystyle\colim_{p\to\infty}M(A(p)^{\mathrm{el}})
. This is a filtered colimit, so it will be enough to show that every
\underline{t}
-morphism
f
of
X
appears at some point in
M(A(p)^{\mathrm{el}})_{\underline{t}}
for some
p
. Indeed, by the Segal condition, it is sufficient for the elementary components
\{f_{a}\}_{a=1,\dotsc,b}
of
f
(the order is irrelevant, the important thing is that all of them are listed) to appear at some stage; since each of those appears at some stage
p_{a}
by the assumption that
A
generates
X
, we can take
p=\max\{p_{a}\}
.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
and let
A\hookrightarrow X_{\underline{1^{n}}}
generate
X
under composition. Then the canonical map

\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{Map}(M(A(\underline{1^{n}})^{\mathrm{el}}),Y)

is a monomorphism of spaces.

Proof.

A map
F:X\to Y
is uniquely determined by its compatible components
F_{\underline{t}}:X_{\underline{t}}\to Y_{\underline{t}}
(see, for example, the end formula for natural transformations in
[
GHN17
, Proposition 5.1]
). We will show that each of those components is uniquely determined by its restriction to
M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})_{\underline{t}}
. For notational simplicity we will fix
n
to be
1
(so that the tuple
\underline{t}
becomes just
t
), trusting that the reader won’t have any troubles extending the argument to an arbitrary
n
.

By naturality and the Segal condition, the map
F_{t}:X_{t}\to Y_{t}
is uniquely determined by the maps
F_{1}
and
F_{0}
; the latter is also determined by
F_{1}
via the map
\mathrm{id}(-):X_{0}\to X_{1}
, so we will focus on the former. Since
X_{1}\simeq\displaystyle\colim_{p\to\infty}A(p)
we have that
F_{1}
is determined by
F_{1}(p):A(p)\to Y_{1}
. Consider now the composition map

\underbrace{A(1)\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}A(1)}_{\text{$p$ copies}}\to X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}\simeq X_{p}\to X_{1}

whose essential image is
A(p)
. Postcomposing with
F_{1}
and using the Segal condition on
Y
gives a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{A(1)\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}A(1)}_{\text{$p$ copies}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{Y_{1}\times_{Y_{0}}\dotsb\times_{Y_{0}}Y_{1}}_{\text{$p$ copies}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(1)^{\times p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}(p)}

which implies that
F_{1}(p)
is uniquely determined by
F_{1}(1)
. Combining these observations, we deduce that
F:X\to Y
is uniquely determined by
F|_{M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})}:M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})\to Y
, as needed.
∎

4.2.
Adjoints from zigzags of commutative squares

Fix a Segal space
X
. We want to apply the above results to the case of
\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{+}(X)
so that we can simplify the space of maps out of
\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{+}(X)
. Before tackling
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
directly, let’s start by understanding
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
. Any
1
-morphism
f:x\to y
of
X
gives rise to two special
(1,1)
-morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
, depicted as commutative squares
\square^{2}\to X
in
X
:

E_{f}:=\,\hbox to72.2pt{\vbox to50.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.09947pt\lower-22.56944pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

By pasting the squares together we can see that they satisfy

E_{f}\circ_{1}H_{f}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{f}\circ_{2}H_{f}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{1}(f^{\downarrow})

where
f^{\to}
and
f^{\downarrow}
are, respectively, the
(1,0)
-morphism and the
(0,1)
-morphism in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
induced by the
1
-morphism
f
of
X
. In this situation say that
f^{\to}
and
f^{\downarrow}
are
companions
, which is a notion that can be defined for arbitrary double Segal spaces (
5.2
). In fact,
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
is universal with respect to those double Segal spaces that admit certain companions. We will discuss this in more detail in
Section

5
.

Remark 4.6
.

Part of proving the universality of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
involves showing that the
(1,1)
-morphisms of the form
E_{f}
,
H_{f}
, and
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f)
generate all
(1,1)
-morphisms under composition. This follows from a straightforward calculation: for any commutative square determined by
g\circ_{1}f\simeq k\circ_{1}h
in
X
we have

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\simeq\quad(\mathrm{id}_{2}(k^{\to})\circ_{1}E_{h})\circ_{2}(H_{g}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})).

Analogous results hold in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)
: for every
1
-morphism
f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}
of
X^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}
(where we use the superscript
(-)^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}
to denote that
f
is the original
1
-morphism in
X
) there are
2
-morphisms
E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}
and
H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}
satisfying
H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}((f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}})^{\to})
and
E_{f_{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{2}H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{1}((f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}})^{\downarrow})
, and these
(1,1)
-morphisms plus the identities generate the whole space of
(1,1)
-morphisms under composition. For notational convenience we will use
f^{\leftarrow}
to denote
(f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}})^{\to}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For any
1
-morphism
f:x\to y
in
X
there is an adjunction
f^{\to}\dashv f^{\leftarrow}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
with unit
\eta=H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}H_{f}
and counit
\varepsilon=E_{f}\circ_{1}E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}
.

Proof.

The first step is to ensure that
f^{\to}
,
f^{\leftarrow}
,
\eta
, and
\varepsilon
are valid morphisms in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
. Recall that

\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X):=(R^{2}_{2}\mathbb{L}_{2}R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1})\left(Z^{2}_{+}(X)\right),\qquad Z^{2}_{+}(X)_{\underline{t}}:=\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{\underline{t}}\sqcup_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{0+}(X)_{\underline{t}}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{\underline{t}}.

The space of
1
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
contains the space
Z^{2}_{+}(X)_{1,0}
and so, in particular,
f^{\to}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,0}
and
f^{\leftarrow}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,0}
are valid
1
-morphisms. The space of
(1,1)
-morphisms of
\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X)
contains
Z^{2}_{+}(X)_{1,1}
, hence it contains
H_{f},E_{f}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}
and
H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}},E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,1}
and therefore it contains the (free) compositions
\eta
and
\varepsilon
. Note also that
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are already globular: if
\partial_{1}^{0}f\simeq x
and
\partial_{1}^{1}f\simeq y
then

\partial_{1}^{0}\eta\simeq\partial_{1}^{0}H_{f}\simeq\mathrm{id}^{2}(x)\simeq\partial_{1}^{1}H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\simeq\partial_{1}^{1}\eta,\qquad\partial_{1}^{0}\varepsilon\simeq\partial_{1}^{0}E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\simeq\mathrm{id}^{2}(y)\simeq\partial_{1}^{1}E_{f}\simeq\partial_{1}^{1}\varepsilon.

Therefore
\eta,\varepsilon\in(R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X))_{1,1}
and so
\eta,\varepsilon\in\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)_{1,1}
.

Now we have to show that the snake equations hold. The argument is the same for both equations, so we will only write down one of them. More precisely, we will prove that there is some
\alpha\in(\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X))_{1,2}
whose components are

\alpha_{01}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\circ_{1}\eta,\quad\alpha_{12}\simeq\varepsilon\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to}),\quad\alpha_{02}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to}),

where
\alpha_{ij}\in(\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X))_{1,1}
denotes the restriction of
\alpha
along the map
\{i,j\}\to[2]
in the second coordinate; moreover, each
\alpha_{ij}
is globular (as can be seen by computing the vertical boundaries) and so
\alpha
belongs to
(R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X))_{1,2}
. The existence of this
\alpha
is enough to prove the snake equations because, in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
,
\alpha
is turned into a witness for an equivalence
\alpha_{12}\circ_{2}\alpha_{01}\simeq\alpha_{02}
.

Consider the following points of
Z^{2}_{+}(X)_{1,2}
:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle:=(H_{f},\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to}))\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}\times_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,0}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,2},

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle:=(H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}},E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}})\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,1}\times_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,0}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,1}\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,2},

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\rightarrow}),E_{f})\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}\times_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,0}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,2},

where we have encoded a point
\gamma
of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{\pm,+}(X)_{1,2}
as the pair
(\gamma_{01},\gamma_{12})
consisting of its two components in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{\pm,+}(X)_{1,1}
(we can do this since
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(-)
is a double Segal space). Note that we have

\partial_{1}^{1}\beta_{1}\simeq(f^{\downarrow},\mathrm{id}^{2}(y))\simeq\partial_{1}^{0}\beta_{2},\qquad\partial_{1}^{1}\beta_{2}\simeq(\mathrm{id}^{2}(x),f^{\downarrow})\simeq\partial_{1}^{0}\beta_{3}

in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{0+}(X)_{0,2}
and so we can form the horizontal composition
\alpha:=\beta_{3}\circ_{1}\beta_{2}\circ_{1}\beta_{1}
in
\mathbb{L}_{1}Z^{2}_{+}(X)
. Since
\alpha\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,2}\times_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{0,2}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,2}\times_{\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{0,2}}\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,2}
, the formula for
\mathbb{L}_{1}
in
2.21
tells us that we can compute the components of
\alpha
as the free composition of the components of the
\beta_{i}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\alpha_{01}

\displaystyle\simeq(\beta_{3})_{01}\circ_{1}(\beta_{2})_{01}\circ_{1}(\beta_{1})_{01}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\circ_{1}H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}H_{f}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\circ_{1}\eta,

\displaystyle\alpha_{12}

\displaystyle\simeq(\beta_{3})_{12}\circ_{1}(\beta_{2})_{12}\circ_{1}(\beta_{1})_{12}\simeq E_{f}\circ_{1}E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\simeq\varepsilon\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to}),

\displaystyle\alpha_{02}

\displaystyle\simeq(\beta_{3})_{02}\circ_{1}(\beta_{2})_{02}\circ_{1}(\beta_{1})_{02}\simeq E_{f}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{1}(f^{\downarrow})\circ_{1}H_{f}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to}),

which is exactly what we needed.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Here is a short diagrammatic explanation of the proof of
4.7
and why it’s not as straightforward as it could be. Write the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

representing a formal vertical composite of two
2
-morphisms in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
, with the top row representing
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})\circ_{1}\eta
and the bottom row representing
\varepsilon\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})
. If we didn’t have to worry about globularity then we could simply use the interchange law for double Segal spaces, which holds in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
, to compute the vertical composition of each column first and then compose the resulting
(1,1)
-morphisms horizontally. In our setting, however, we can only perform vertical compositions of
globular

(1,1)
-morphisms (as the
\mathbb{L}_{2}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)=(R_{2}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{2}R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2})(X)
comes
after
the
R_{1}^{2}
), and the columns of the diagram are not globular. Fortunately we can show that there is a pre-existing relation in
(R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2})(X)
which allows us to bypass the interchange law and still obtain the desired result.

4.3.
Generators for zigzags

We have just shown that the canonical inclusion
X\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
promotes every
1
-morphism of
X
to a left adjoint. We will now prove that the (co)units of these adjunctions are enough to generate
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
under composition.

Lemma 4.9
.

Every
2
-morphism of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
can be written as a vertical composition of zigzags
\sigma_{k}\circ_{1}\sigma_{k-1}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}\sigma_{1}
, where

(a)

each
\sigma_{i}
is in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}
or
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{1,1}
,

(b)

\partial_{1}^{0}\sigma_{1}
and
\partial_{1}^{1}\sigma_{k}
are identities, and

(c)

the compositions
\circ_{1}
are computed in
\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2}(X)
.

Proof.

This is a restatement of the formula for
\mathbb{L}_{1}
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}
in
2.21
and the placement of these functors in the definition
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)=(R_{2}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{2}R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2})(X)
. Namely, first we use
\mathbb{L}_{1}
to form the zigzags, then
R_{1}^{2}
to ensure that each zigzag is globular, and then we use
\mathbb{L}_{2}
to compose these zigzags freely.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Each zigzag
\sigma_{k}\circ_{1}\sigma_{k-1}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}\sigma_{1}
as above can be written, in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
, as a composition of
2
-morphisms of the form
\eta_{f}=H_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}H_{f}
,
\varepsilon_{f}=E_{f}\circ_{1}E_{f^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}
,
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})
, or
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\leftarrow})
.

Proof.

We will use the same strategy as in the proof of
4.7
: we will find some
\alpha\in(R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2})(X)_{1,2}
such that
\alpha_{01}
and
\alpha_{12}
are free horizontal compositions of things of the form
\eta_{f}
,
\varepsilon_{f}
,
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})
, or
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\leftarrow})
and such that
\alpha_{02}
is the zigzag. Without loss of generality we may assume that the
(1,1)
-morphisms
\sigma_{i}
alternate between being in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)
and
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)
, i.e. we compose everything that is already composable in the starting double categories. To simplify the proof we will assume that
k=2j
is even and that
\sigma_{1}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{1,1}
, but exactly the same argument will produce a proof for the other three cases.

First write every
\sigma_{2i-1}
and
\sigma_{2i}
, for
i=1,\dotsc,j
, as commutative squares:

\sigma_{2i-1}\simeq\,\hbox to81.03pt{\vbox to52.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.51208pt\lower-24.6097pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-39.4959pt}{-19.88197pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

Since the pairs
(\sigma_{2i-1},\sigma_{2i})
and
(\sigma_{2i},\sigma_{2i+1})
are composable we must have
\hat{g}_{i}\simeq g_{i}
and
\hat{h}_{i}\simeq h_{i+1}
for all valid values of
i
. Now use
4.6
to write

\sigma_{2i-1}\simeq(\mathrm{id}_{2}(k_{i}^{\to})\circ_{1}E_{h_{i}})\circ_{2}(H_{g_{i}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f_{i}^{\to})),\qquad\sigma_{2i}\simeq(E_{\hat{h}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{k}_{i}^{\leftarrow}))\circ_{2}(\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{f}_{i}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}H_{\hat{g}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}).

In particular we have
\alpha_{2i-1}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{++}(X)_{12}
and
\alpha_{2i}\in\mathrm{Sq}^{2}_{-+}(X)_{12}
such that

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i-1})_{01}

\displaystyle\simeq H_{g_{i}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f_{i}^{\to}),

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i})_{01}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{f}_{i}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}H_{\hat{g}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i-1})_{12}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(k_{i}^{\to})\circ_{1}E_{h_{i}},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i})_{12}

\displaystyle\simeq E_{\hat{h}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{k}_{i}^{\leftarrow}),

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i-1})_{02}

\displaystyle\simeq\sigma_{2i-1},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2i})_{02}

\displaystyle\simeq\sigma_{2i},

and so we have
\alpha:=\alpha_{2j}\circ_{1}\alpha_{2j-1}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}\alpha_{2}\circ_{1}\alpha_{1}\in(\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2}(X))_{1,2}
. By assumption the zigzag
\sigma_{k}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}\sigma_{1}
is globular and therefore
h_{1}
and
\hat{h}_{j}
are identities, which also implies that
\alpha
is globular, i.e.
\alpha\in(R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{2}(X))_{1,2}
. Finally, we must verify that (
\dagger
)
\alpha_{01}
and
\alpha_{02}
are composites of (co)units and identities and that (
\ddagger
)
\alpha_{02}
is the zigzag we started with. Notice that for any
a,b\in\{0,1,2\}
we have

\alpha_{ab}\simeq(\alpha_{2j})_{ab}\circ_{1}(\alpha_{2j-1})_{ab}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}(\alpha_{2})_{ab}\circ_{1}(\alpha_{1})_{ab}

by the formula in
2.21
; this immediately implies (
\ddagger
). For (
\dagger
), note that

(\alpha_{2j})_{01}\circ_{1}(\alpha_{2j-1})_{01}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}(\alpha_{1})_{01}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{f}_{j}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}\eta_{g_{j}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f_{j}^{\to})\dotsb\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{f}_{1}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}\eta_{g_{1}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(f_{1}^{\to})

since
\hat{g}_{i}\simeq g_{i}
and

(\alpha_{2j})_{12}\circ_{1}(\alpha_{2j-1})_{12}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}(\alpha_{1})_{12}\simeq E_{\hat{h}_{j}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{k}_{j}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(k_{j}^{\to})\circ_{1}\varepsilon_{h_{j}}\circ_{1}\dotsb\circ_{1}\varepsilon_{h_{1}}\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(\hat{k}_{j}^{\leftarrow})\circ_{1}\mathrm{id}_{2}(k_{j}^{\to})\circ_{1}E_{h_{1}}.

Together with the fact that
E_{h_{1}}
and
E_{\hat{h}_{j}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}}
are identities (since
h_{1}
and
\hat{h}_{j}
are identities) these two equations prove (
\ddagger
), and so we’re done.
∎

4.9
and
4.10
allow us to conclude the following generation result:

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
A\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{2}(X)_{1}
denote the space containing all the
2
-morphisms of the form

(1)

\mathrm{id}(\mathrm{id}(x))
for
x\in X_{0}
,

(2)

\mathrm{id}(f^{\to})
and
\mathrm{id}(f^{\leftarrow})
for all
f\in X_{1}
,

(3)

\eta_{f}
and
\varepsilon_{f}
, as defined in
4.10
, for all
f\in X_{1}
.

Then
A
generates
\mathcal{Z}^{2}(X)
under composition.

Corollary 4.12
.

If
\mathscr{D}
is a sinister
2
-category and
F:X\to\mathscr{D}
is a map from a Segal space, the induced map
\widetilde{F}:\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
factors through the canonical inclusion
\mathscr{D}\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
.

Proof.

It’s enough to show that the generating morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
are sent to morphisms in
\mathscr{D}
. This is obvious for objects. Morphisms coming from
X
are sent to morphisms in
\mathscr{D}
by construction. If
f^{\leftarrow}
is a morphism coming from
X^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}
then
\widetilde{F}(f^{\leftarrow})\simeq(F(f))^{\leftarrow}
, and the latter is equivalent to any right adjoint
g
of
F(f)
in
\mathscr{D}
via the invertible
2
-morphism represented by the zigzag

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
. The same holds for the (co)units, since
\widetilde{F}(E_{f})
and
\widetilde{F}(H_{f})
are equivalent to the (co)units
\varepsilon
and
\eta
of an adjunction
F(f)\dashv g
using
[
RV16
, Theorem 4.4.18]
.
∎

4.4.
Universal property

In this subsection we will prove our main result:

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a sinister
(\infty,2)
-category. Then, for any Segal space
X
, the map

i^{\ast}:\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X),\mathscr{D})\to\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{Map}(X,\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})

obtained by precomposing with the canonical inclusion
i:X\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
is an equivalence of spaces.

Proof.

To show that
i^{\ast}
is a monomorphism, let
F:\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor and consider
E:=i^{\ast}(F)
. By
4.11
the map

\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{+}(X),\mathscr{D})\to\mathrm{Map}(M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}}),\mathscr{D})

is a monomorphism; recall that
A
contains all of
X_{0}
,
X_{1}
,
X_{1}^{\mathrm{op}_{1}}
, and the (co)units of the adjunctions
f^{\to}\dashv f^{\leftarrow}
. Therefore the inclusion
X\to\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{+}(X)
factors through
M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}})
, and we can ask whether the map

\mathrm{Map}(M(A(1)^{\mathrm{el}}),\mathscr{D})\to\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{Map}(X,\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})

is a monomorphism, which will imply that
i^{\ast}
is a monomorphism. The fiber of this map over a functor
E:X\to\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}
is seen to be equivalent to
\prod_{f\in X_{1}}\mathrm{Adj}_{\mathscr{D}}(E(f))
, where, for any
g\in\mathscr{D}_{1,0}

\mathrm{Adj}_{\mathscr{D}}(g)\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Adj},\mathscr{D})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{1,0}}\{g\}

is the space of adjunction data that have
g
as a left adjoint. But since
\mathscr{D}
is sinister each of these spaces is non-empty and, therefore, contractible by
[
RV16
, Theorem 4.4.18]
. Hence the fiber over
E
is contractible, as desired.

For essential surjectivity, we will produce an explicit section of
i^{\ast}
on path components. First note that there is a map
p:\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{D}
such that its restriction to
\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}
is the inclusion
j:\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{D}
: indeed,
j
induces a map
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
which factors as
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{D}\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\mathscr{D})
thanks to
4.12
, and the restriction to
\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}
returns
j
by construction. Now we have a map

p_{\ast}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}:\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}(X,\tau_{1}\mathscr{D})\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X),\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}))\to\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X),\mathscr{D})

such that
i^{\ast}\circ p_{\ast}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}\simeq p_{\ast}\circ i^{\ast}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{id}
, i.e.
p_{\ast}\circ\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}
is a section of
i^{\ast}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

The following are immediate consequences of
4.13
:

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be any
(\infty,2)
-category and let
\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ladj}}\subseteq\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}
denote the full sub-
\infty
-category containing the
1
-morphisms which are left adjoints in
\mathscr{D}
. Then the inclusion
X\to\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X),\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{Map}(X,\tau_{1}\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ladj}}).

Corollary 4.15
.

There is an equivalence
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}([1])\simeq\mathrm{Adj}
between the zigzagification of the walking arrow and the free adjunction.

The last corollary can be gleamed from the graphical representation of the
2
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}([1])
. Indeed, in this setting we have exactly two generating
2
-morphisms,
\eta
and
\varepsilon
, the unit and counit for the adjunction
f^{\to}\dashv f^{\leftarrow}
. On top of the zigzags representing
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\to})
,
\mathrm{id}_{2}(f^{\leftarrow})
,
\mathrm{id}_{1}(f)
,
\eta
and
\varepsilon
we can draw lines connecting the midpoints of the arrows in each commutative diagram, as in
Figure

1
.

and the other is similar. After pasting these drawings together using zigzags we re-obtain the graphical calculus for
\mathrm{Adj}
of
[
RV16
, Section 3.1]
and of
[
DPP03
, Section 6, Example 2]
.

5.
Higher-dimensional behavior of zigzagification

We conclude the paper with a speculative section where we provide a few observations about the general case of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
for
X\in\mathrm{Glb}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
and hint at a possible universal property.

5.1.
Adjoints from zigzags of lax commutative
(n+1)
-cubes

Recall briefly how we built right adjoints of
1
-morphisms
f\in X_{1}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{2}(X)
: we first produced a companionship for
f^{\to}
in
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
, which in particular gave us commutative squares
E_{f}
and
H_{f}
, and then glued those commutative squares with their horizontal opposites to obtain (co)units
\varepsilon
and
\eta
for the adjunction
f^{\to}\dashv f^{\leftarrow}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Every
k
-morphism
\alpha
of
X
has a right adjoint in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
.

The proof of this statement is similar to that of
4.7
. By induction, using
3.30
, the low-dimensional adjunctions (
k<n
) are taken care of in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+1}(\tau_{k}X)
, so we can assume
k=n
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
Y\in\mathrm{Seg}^{n+1}(\mathfrak{S})
.

•

If
n=1
, a
(
1
-dimensional) companionship
in
Y
is the data of
f\in X_{1,0}
,
g\in X_{0,1}
, and
H,E\in X_{1,1}
satisfying
E\circ_{1}H\simeq\mathrm{id}_{1}(g)
and
E\circ_{2}H\simeq\mathrm{id}_{2}(f)
.

•

if
n\geq 2
, a
(
n
-dimensional) companionship
is a companionship in the double Segal space
Y_{1,\dotsc,1,\bullet,\bullet}
.

Remark 5.3
.

The content of
4.6
is that every
(1,0)
-morphism of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
extends to a
1
-dimensional companionship.

Proposition 5.4
.

If
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(\mathfrak{S})
then every
(\underline{1^{n}},0)
-morphism of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
extends to an
n
-dimensional companionship. In particular, every
n
-morphism
\alpha
of
X
has a right adjoint
\alpha^{\leftarrow}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
.

Proof.

Using the fact that the Gray tensor product has a right adjoint (see
[
Cam23
]
), say
A\otimes-\dashv R_{A}
, we see that
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)_{\underline{1^{n-1}},\bullet,\bullet}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(\tau_{2}R_{\square(n-1)}(X))
. By definition,
n
-dimensional companionships in the former are
1
-dimensional companionships in the latter, and so we’re done by
5.3
.

An
n
-morphism
\alpha
of
X
induces
(\underline{1^{n}},0)
-morphisms
\alpha^{\to}
of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
and
\alpha^{\leftarrow}
of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)^{\mathrm{op}_{n}}
, together with a companionship between
\alpha^{\to}
and the
(\underline{1^{n-1}},0,1)
-morphism
\alpha^{\downarrow}
in
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
. Note that
\alpha^{\to}
and
\alpha^{\leftarrow}
are globular up to height
n
and
\alpha^{\downarrow}
is globular up to height
n-1
, meaning that the companionship extends to
(R_{n-1}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{n-1}\dotsb R_{1}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{1}Z_{+}^{n+1})(X)
. Now the same argument used in
4.7
(with
R_{1}^{2}\mathbb{L}_{1}
replaced by
R_{n}^{n+1}\mathbb{L}_{n}
) shows that the the companionship data for
\alpha^{\to}
gives two
(n+1)
-morphisms
\varepsilon_{\alpha}
and
\eta_{\alpha}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
satisfying the snake equations.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

For
k\leq n-1
, every
k
-morphism
\alpha
of
X
has an ambidextrous adjoint in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
: the
n
-morphism
\alpha^{\leftarrow}
satisfies
\alpha^{\to}\dashv\alpha^{\leftarrow}
and
\alpha^{\leftarrow}\dashv\alpha^{\to}
.

Proof.

The construction of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
is invariant under the application of
(-)^{\mathrm{op}_{k}}
for
k\leq n-1
, meaning that
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)^{\mathrm{op}_{k}}\simeq\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
. This is because
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},+}(X)^{\mathrm{op}_{k}}\simeq\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b^{\prime}},+}(X)
with
b_{i}^{\prime}=b_{i}
for
i\neq k
and
b_{k}^{\prime}=-b_{k}
. Under these equivalences,
\alpha^{\to}
is sent to
\alpha^{\leftarrow}
and viceversa. But in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)^{\mathrm{op}_{k}}
we have
\alpha^{\leftarrow}\dashv\alpha^{\to}
, and so the claim follows.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

In fact, we believe that, for
k\leq n-1
,
every

k
-morphism of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
has an ambidextrous adjoint. Such
k
-morphisms are given by taking
k
zigzags of lax commutative
k
-cubes in
\tau_{k}X
of signature
(\underline{b},c)\in\Lambda^{k}
. Each cube has a counterpart obtained by considering the same cube but with signature
(\underline{b},-c)\in\Lambda^{k}
. Rewriting the
k
zigzags with the counterparts yields the desired adjoint, with (co)units obtained using the companionships of each cube.

5.2.
On generators

The proof of
4.10
depends on a certain decomposition result for
(1,1)
-morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
, which is one part of the following universal property of
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}
: praphrasing the results of
[
LR25
]
(and deliberately omitting issues of completeness for simplicity’s sake),
\mathrm{Sq}^{2}(X)
is initial among those double Segal spaces which admit a map from
X
that sends every
1
-morphism
f
to a
(1,0)
-morphism with a companion.

It follows that if we wanted to prove a generators-and-relations result for
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
we could start by showing a similar universal property for
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}
. At the moment, however, it is unclear what such a property would say. In
5.2
we defined an
n
-dimensional companionship for an
(n+1)
-uple Segal
Y
to be a companionship in
Y_{1,\dotsc,1,\bullet,\bullet}
, but we could reasonably form different double Segal spaces out of
Y
, such as
Y_{\bullet,\bullet,1,\dotsc,1}
or
Y_{\bullet,1,\dotsc,1,\bullet}
, and ask for companionships there. Let’s call them
alternative companionships
to distinguish them from those of
5.2
. It turns out that, in general,
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
admits
some
alternative companionships. For example, in
\mathrm{Sq}^{3}(X)
we have that every
(1,0,1)
-morphism admits a companion
(0,1,1)
-morphism; not every
(1,1,0)
-morphism admits a companion
(0,1,1)
-morphism, but those
(1,1,0)
-morphisms that are globular up to height
1
(i.e. that come from
2
-morphisms of
X
) do. In fact, implicit in the proof of
5.1
is the fact that every
k
-morphism of
X
, when considered as a
(\underline{1^{k}},\underline{0^{n+1-k}})
-morphism of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
via the canonical inclusion, admits a companion
(\underline{1^{k-1}},0,1,\underline{0^{n-k}})
-morphism.

The evidence from low dimensional cases and some wishful thinking leads us to formulate the following

Conjecture 5.7
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(X)
and let
k\leq n+1
. The space of
(\underline{1^{k}},\underline{0^{n+1-k}})
-morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
is generated under composition by the
k
-morphisms of
X
and the companionship (co)units for lower-dimensional morphisms of
X
.

The claim would be but one step in the proof of a possible universal property for
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
, stating something along the lines of “
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}(X)
is initial among those
(n+1)
-uple Segal spaces which admit a map from
X
sending every
k
-morphism to a
(\underline{1^{k}},\underline{0^{n+1-k}})
-morphism with a (suitable) companion”.

For our purposes, the claim gives us a grasp on the
k
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
when
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n+1}(X)
: since the companionship (co)units in each
\mathrm{Sq}^{n+1}_{\underline{b},+}(X)
glue together to form (co)units for adjunctions of
k
-morphisms in
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
, we expect the following:

Conjecture 5.8
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{GlbSeg}^{n,n}(X)
and let
k\leq n+1
. Then the space of
k
-morphisms of
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)
is generated under composition by the
k
-morphisms of
X
and the adjunction (co)units for lower-dimensional morphisms of
X
.

According to the conjecture, maps
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)\to\mathscr{D}
to a sinister
(n+1)
-category are determined by their restriction to
X
. It remains a mystery if we can go the other way, i.e. if we can produce maps
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n+1}(X)\to\mathscr{D}
from maps
X\to\mathscr{D}
, because we have little to no knowledge about the relations among adjunction (co)units in dimensions
n\geq 2
.

5.3.
Relation to cobordisms and future work

As mentioned in
Section

1
, our initial motivation was to produce a combinatorial construction for the cobordism higher category
\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{fr}}
. It turns out that
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{n}
can be used to approximate a different but closely related higher category, the
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category of embedded oriented tangles. Here is a brief explanation of how this works. More details on this topic will be presented in future work.

Consider
F^{k}
, the free
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-algebra in
\mathfrak{S}
on one generator. Under the equivalence between
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-algebras and
k
-fold monoids in cartesian monoidal
\infty
-categories (see, for example,
[
Hau18
, Proposition 10.11]
),
F^{k}
induces a globular
k
-uple Segal space
B^{k}F^{k}
with a contractible space of
j
-morphisms for all
0\leq j<k
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+n}(B^{k}F^{k})
is a globular
(k+n)
-uple Segal space with a contractible space of
j
-morphisms for all
0\leq j<k
, and thus it induces an
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal globular
n
-uple Segal space
T^{k,n}:=\Omega^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+n}(B^{k}F^{k})
.

We claim that the
j
-morphisms of
T^{k,n}
can be turned into the data of compact oriented
j
-dimensional manifolds with corners. First note that
F^{k}
can be modeled by the space
\mathrm{Conf}(k)
of embeddings of points in
(0,1)^{k}
, the open unit
k
-cube; the
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal structure is given by pasting two cubes along a face and then rescaling. Paths in this space are isotopies of embeddings, paths between paths are isotopies between isotopies, and so on. In particular, any map
f:[0,1]^{r}\to\mathrm{Conf}(k)
from the closed
r
-cube can be “realized” as an oriented manifold: first factor
f
as a map
[0,1]^{r}\to\mathrm{Emb}(m,(0,1)^{k})
into the space of embeddings of
m
points
\{p_{1},\dotsc,p_{m}\}
into
(0,1)^{k}
, and then define

M_{f}:=\{(x,y)\subseteq[0,1]^{r}\times(0,1)^{k}\mid y=f(x)(p_{i})\text{ for some }i=1,\dotsc,m\}\subseteq[0,1]^{r}\times(0,1)^{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r+k}

Note that
M_{f}
is abstractly diffeomorphic to the disjoint union of
m
copies of
[0,1]^{r}
, but as a manifold with corners it might be non-trivial.

Now consider a map
\alpha:\square^{k+j}\to B^{k}F^{k}\simeq B^{k}\mathrm{Conf}(k)
of
(\infty,k+j)
-categories. Composing with the inclusion
w(k+j)\to\square^{k+j}
gives us a specific
(k+j)
-morphism of
B^{k}\mathrm{Conf}(k)
. For
j=0
this corresponds to a point of
\mathrm{Conf}(k)
, for
j=1
this is a path in
\mathrm{Conf}(k)
, for
j=2
it’s a path between paths, and so on. In general it corresponds to a certain map
a:[0,1]^{j}\to\mathrm{Conf}(k)
and thus, via the realization, a compact oriented
j
-dimensional manifold with corners
M_{a}
. The boundary of
M_{a}
is given by the realization of the faces of
\square^{k+j}
along
\alpha
.

The discussion above can be summarized by saying that
(k+j)
-dimensional morphisms of
\mathrm{Sq}^{k+j}(B^{k}F^{k})
can be turned into compact oriented
j
-dimensional manifolds with corners embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{k+j}
. Similarly, a
(k+j)
-dimensional morphism
\alpha
of
\mathrm{Sq}^{k+j}_{\underline{b}}(B^{k}F^{k})
gives the same manifold
M_{a}
as the corresponding morphism
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Sq}^{k+j}(B^{k}F^{k})
but we introduce a “formal orientation” depending on the parity of
\sum_{i}b_{i}
: if the latter is even then the orientation of
M_{a}
matches that of
M_{a^{\prime}}
and if the latter is odd then the orientation of
M_{a}
is the opposite one.

Ultimately, our hope is the following:

Conjecture 5.9
.

The above association from maps
\square^{r}\to B^{k}F^{k}
to manifolds yields a map
T^{k,n}=\Omega^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{+}^{k+n}(B^{k}F^{k})\to\mathrm{Tang}_{k,n}^{\mathrm{or}}
into the
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category of oriented tangles defined in
[
AF17
]
.

Analyzing this map might provide more insight into the
tangle hypothesis
, an
\mathbb{E}_{k}
-monoidal analogue of the cobordism hypothesis, formulated in
[
AF17
]
and proven conditionally on a different conjecture about factorization homology. As explained in the introduction, taking colimits would yield a map
\colim_{k}T^{k,n}\to\mathrm{Bord}_{n}^{\mathrm{or}}
that might help us understand the oriented version of the cobordism hypothesis.
